Abstract: "long run stochastic stability" is related to the speed of evolution toward and away from a convention; evolution is faster "when it may proceed via a series of small steps between intermediate steady states"; the radius and modified coradius "characterize the long run stochastically stable set" and "bound the speed with which evolutionary change occurs"; risk dominance in 2×2 games is generalised to "½-dominant equilibria in arbitrary games"; applications include two-dimensional local interaction and cycles as stochastically stable sets [V] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/oup/restud/v67y2000i1p17-45..html)). [M]: the theorem states that if R(Ω) > CR*(Ω) for a union of limit sets Ω, the stochastically stable set lies in Ω and the expected waiting time to reach Ω is O(ε^{−CR*(Ω)}) as ε → 0.
- Example protocols (Sandholm AMS notes, Examples 5.1–5.2) [V] ([Sandholm AMS](https://users.ssc.wisc.edu/~whs/research/ams.pdf)): best response with mutations BRM(ε): switch to the best response with probability 1 − ε, uniform at random with probability ε (two strategies: optimal with probability 1 − ε/2) (KMR 1993; Young 1993); logit ρ_ij(π) = exp(η^{-1}(π_j − π_{k*}))/Σ_k exp(η^{-1}(π_k − π_{k*})) (Blume 1993, 1997). Under BRM mistakes are independent of their cost; under logit costlier mistakes are exponentially rarer, and "predictions of infinite horizon behavior hinge on the relative probabilities of rare events".
- Birth–death stationary distribution, exact (Theorem 5.3) [V] ([Sandholm AMS](https://users.ssc.wisc.edu/~whs/research/ams.pdf)): N agents, two-strategy game F, full-support protocol ρ; on X^N = {0, 1/N, ..., 1},
  μ^N_x / μ^N_0 = Π_{j=1}^{Nx} [(N − j + 1)/j] · ρ_01(F((j−1)/N), (j−1)/N) / ρ_10(F(j/N), j/N),
  normalised to sum to 1. It follows from detailed balance μ_x q_x = μ_{x−1/N} p_{x−1/N} for birth–death chains (eq. 16).
- Small-noise vs. large-population limits (Theorem 7.7) [V] (same source): define the relative cost κ̃(d) = lim_{η→0}(−η log σ_η(−d) + η log σ_η(d)), the ordinal potential I(x) = ∫_0^x κ̃(F_Δ(y)) dy with F_Δ = F_1 − F_0, and ΔI(x) = I(x) − max_y I(y). Then lim_{N→∞} lim_{η→0} max_x |(η/N) log μ^{N,η}_x − ΔI(x)| = 0 and the same with the limits reversed: the two orders agree. For BRM, I is the signum potential I_sgn(x) = ∫_0^x sgn(F_Δ(y)) dy = |x − x*| − x* in a coordination game with mixed equilibrium x*; for logit, I is the standard potential ∫_0^x F_Δ(y) dy; for probit, the quadratic potential ∫_0^x ¼⟨F_Δ(y)⟩² dy.
- Risk dominance and stochastic stability (Corollaries 7.11–7.12) [V] (same source): in a two-strategy coordination game with linear payoffs, e_i is weakly stochastically stable under every noisy best response protocol iff strategy i is weakly risk dominant (x* > ½ makes strategy 0 strictly risk dominant), and uniquely stochastically stable if strictly risk dominant. Under BRM the same holds for nonlinear payoffs; under other rules with nonlinear payoffs risk dominance is "no longer a necessary or sufficient condition"; Theorem 7.14 replaces it by a stochastic-dominance condition.
- Practical scale (same source, §5.4): with N = 100, a mutation rate ε = 10⁻⁵ is not small enough for the small-noise prediction, ε = 10⁻⁷ is closer; "in economic contexts ... the large population limit is more likely to be the relevant one" when the two limits disagree [V].
- Reversibility beyond two strategies [V] (same source, §8): "The only many-strategy evolutionary game environments known to generate reversible processes are potential games ... with agents using either the standard or imitative versions of the logit choice rule" (Blume 1993, 1997; Sandholm 2011). Otherwise stationary distributions have no closed form and one uses Freidlin–Wentzell large deviations.
- Gibbs form (Young 2015, Theorem 1 and Corollary 1.1) [V] ([Young 2015 preprint](https://www.econ2.jhu.edu/people/young/SocNormsOCT1.pdf)): for log-linear response to prior-period actions in an n-person game built from a two-person symmetric potential game ρ with weights w_ij and idiosyncratic action values v_i, the potential is ρ*(x) = Σ_{ij} w_ij ρ(x_i, x_j) + Σ_i v_i(x_i), the unique ergodic distribution is μ(x) = e^{βρ*(x)}/Σ_{x′} e^{βρ*(x′)}, and the stochastically stable states are exactly the maximisers of ρ*. Blume (1993), "The statistical mechanics of strategic interaction", *GEB* 5:387–424 [S via reference list; DOI 10.1006/game.1993.1023 M].
- Large-population limit and the mean dynamic (Sandholm 2015, §13.10.1) [V]: states retaining mass as N → ∞ lie in the Birkhoff centre of the mean dynamic (Benaïm and Weibull 2003); Benaïm (1998) excludes unstable rest points under global conditions; locating the selected component needs large deviations (Benaïm, Sandholm, Staudigl 2014).

#### Inferences
- The infrastructure's Markov-chain code can compute exact stationary distributions of the finite-N process for small N and two or three strategies, and compare them with (i) Theorem 5.3's product formula, (ii) the Gibbs formula in potential games, (iii) the predicted selection (risk-dominant convention), and (iv) the rate (η/N) log μ → ΔI. These are deterministic, closed-form checks.
- For non-reversible cases, a resistance-tree (minimum-cost arborescence) solver over the recurrent classes of the unperturbed chain computes stochastic potential exactly on finite state spaces; Ellison's radius/coradius gives a sufficient condition and a waiting-time bound that can be checked by simulation.

#### Gaps
- Wallace and Young (2015) and Young (1998) were not read in full; the adaptive-play sampling condition, the resistance-tree theorem's statement, and Ellison's exact theorem are marked [M].
- No closed-form waiting times (e.g., Kramers-type escape times in finite N) were verified.

### 3. Learning in Games and Its Fit to Experimental Data

#### Takeaway
Fictitious play is the discrete counterpart of the best response dynamic, stochastic fictitious play of perturbed best response dynamics, and Cross-type reinforcement of the replicator; so the population dynamics above double as continuous-time limits of individual learning rules. Empirically, simple reinforcement (Roth–Erev) predicts long-run play in mixed-strategy games better than equilibrium; EWA nests reinforcement and belief learning and generally beats both in-sample and on holdouts; self-tuning EWA matches EWA out of sample with fewer parameters; but simulation studies (Salmon 2001) show standard econometric methods have difficulty telling learning rules apart, which limits how much any fit identifies the rule.

#### Cited Findings
- Fictitious play (Brown 1949, 1951) "is essentially a discrete-time version of the best response dynamic", after a reparametrisation of time; stochastic fictitious play (Fudenberg and Kreps 1993, GEB 5:320–367) is a decreasing-step stochastic process whose expected motion is a perturbed best response dynamic, so stochastic approximation (Benaïm 1999) gives global convergence results (Fudenberg and Kreps 1993; Kaniovski and Young 1995; Benaïm and Hirsch 1999; Hofbauer and Sandholm 2002; Benaïm and Faure 2012; Perkins and Leslie 2013); the rule is ε-consistent [V] ([Sandholm 2015](https://users.ssc.wisc.edu/~whs/teaching/805/handbook.pdf), §13.10.2).
- Shapley (1964) constructed the first example of cycling under fictitious play [V] (same source, Example 13.26).
- Reinforcement and the replicator: Börgers and Sarin (1997, JET 77:1–14) show that Cross's (1973) reinforcement rule yields the replicator dynamic in the continuous-time limit; Hopkins (2002) links reinforcement learning and the logit/replicator dynamics [V] (same source, §13.10.2 and fn. 50). The convergence results for contractive games are inspired by Hart and Mas-Colell's (2001, JET 98:26–54) consistent strategies [V] (same source).
- Erev and Roth (1998), "Predicting how people play games: reinforcement learning in experimental games with unique, mixed strategy equilibria", *AER* 88(4):848–881: data from published experiments with 100 or more periods of play in games with unique mixed equilibria; models assessed both by best fit and by ex ante prediction with parameters estimated on other experiments; "even a one-parameter reinforcement learning model robustly outperforms the equilibrium predictions"; predictive power improves with forgetting and experimentation, or with probabilistic fictitious play [S] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/aea/aecrev/v88y1998i4p848-81.html); [author PDF](https://stanford.edu/%7Ealroth/papers/ENO.pdf)). [M]: the basic model updates propensities q_j(t+1) = (1 − φ)q_j(t) + E_k(j, R(x)) with experimentation ε spilling reinforcement to other strategies, and chooses j with probability q_j/Σ_k q_k; Roth and Erev (1995), *GEB* 8:164–212, is the earlier paper on intermediate-term play.
- Camerer, C. and Ho, T.-H. (1999), "Experience-weighted attraction learning in normal form games", *Econometrica* 67(4):827–874, DOI 10.1111/1468-0262.00054: reinforcement and weighted fictitious play are special cases; two decay parameters govern attractions and experience; fitted to three experimental datasets with calibration on part of the data and prediction on a holdout; the special cases are "generally rejected in favor of EWA, though belief models do better in some constant-sum games" [S] ([Econometric Society](https://econometricsociety.org/publications/econometrica/browse/1999/07/01/experience‐weighted-attraction-learning-normal-form-games); [Caltech](https://authors.library.caltech.edu/records/4e2kf-nme71)). [M] Update: N(t) = ρN(t−1) + 1; A_j(t) = [φN(t−1)A_j(t−1) + (δ + (1 − δ)·1{s_j = s_i(t)})·π_i(s_j, s_{−i}(t))]/N(t); choice by logit with sensitivity λ. δ = 0, ρ = 0 gives cumulative reinforcement; δ = 1, ρ = φ gives weighted fictitious play.
- Ho, T.-H., Camerer, C., Chong, J.K. (2007), "Self-tuning experience weighted attraction learning in games", *JET* 133(1):177–198: parameters replaced by functions of experience; on seven games it "does as well as EWA in predicting behavior in new games" with fewer parameters, and fits reliably better than QRE [S] ([SUFE abstract](https://academicnewsletter.sufe.edu.cn/info/302441)); DOI 10.1016/j.jet.2005.12.008 [M]. A later "functional EWA" working paper reports that fEWA or parametrised EWA predict best out of sample, while one reinforcement model predicts well in mixed-equilibrium games [S] ([Caltech record](https://authors.library.caltech.edu/records/ge461-3d968)).
- Salmon, T.C. (2001), "An evaluation of econometric models of adaptive learning", *Econometrica* 69(6):1597–1628, DOI 10.1111/1468-0262.00258: four econometric approaches estimated on simulated data whose generating rule is known; "all of the models examined have difficulty accurately distinguishing between the data generating processes" [S] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/ecm/emetrp/v69y2001i6p1597-1628.html)).
- Bargaining norms in the laboratory (Gallo's experiment, reported by Young 2015) [V] ([Young 2015 preprint](https://www.econ2.jhu.edu/people/young/SocNormsOCT1.pdf)): buyers on a network sampled the seller's past demands, the seller best-responded with probability 95% and demanded uniformly in {1, ..., 17} with probability 5%; convergence to a norm occurred in 11 of 13 trials, norms from 9 to 14 tokens, average waiting time 33 rounds; subjects' deviation from myopic best response fell as an equilibrium was approached; the stochastically stable norm is predicted to be sensitive to the minimum sample size in a population.

#### Inferences
- A learning module needs one "attraction" interface with EWA as the general case and reinforcement, weighted fictitious play and Cournot best response as parameter settings; the logit choice already present in nomos supplies the choice map. Börgers–Sarin and the fictitious-play/best-response correspondence give convergence checks against the deterministic dynamics.
- Salmon's result suggests that any fitting tool should report how well the record identifies the rule (e.g., a parameter recovery study on simulated data) before reporting which rule fits best, in keeping with the infrastructure's habit of saying what a record does not identify.

#### Gaps
- The magnitude of EWA's improvement (log-likelihoods, hit rates) and Erev and Roth's mean squared deviations were not read.
- Choice prediction competitions (Erev, Ert, Roth and coauthors, 2010 onward) were not checked; their findings on which learning models win are not reported here.
- Hart and Mas-Colell's regret matching (Econometrica 2000) convergence to correlated equilibrium is relevant [M] but was not verified.

### 4. Cultural Evolution of Norms, Conventions and Institutions

#### Takeaway
The economic literature models norms and conventions as equilibria of recurrent games selected by stochastic evolutionary dynamics (Young 1993, 1998, 2015), with three qualitative signatures (punctuated equilibrium, compression, local conformity with global diversity) that have been confronted with data (Illinois share contracts; footbinding). Cultural transmission models (Cavalli-Sforza–Feldman; Boyd–Richerson; Bisin–Verdier) give replicator-like logistic dynamics; Bisin–Verdier's purposeful socialisation with cultural substitution yields a globally stable heterogeneous population with a closed form. Institutional co-evolution with behaviour is studied by multilevel simulation (Bowles, Choi, Hopfensitz 2003); Aoki's comparative institutional analysis treats institutions as shared beliefs that summarise an equilibrium.

#### Cited Findings

**Norms as stochastically stable equilibria (Young)**
- Young (2015), "The evolution of social norms", *Annual Review of Economics* 7:359–387, DOI 10.1146/annurev-economics-080614-115322 [V] ([preprint](https://www.econ2.jhu.edu/people/young/SocNormsOCT1.pdf); [IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/anr/reveco/v7y2015p359-387.html)): "Social norms are patterns of behavior that are self-enforcing at the group level: everyone wants to conform when they expect everyone else to conform"; mechanisms include coordination, fear of sanctions, signalling membership, following others; cases are bargaining, contract terms, retirement, duelling, footbinding, medical treatment and contraception.
- Updating rules in that framework [V] (same source, §4.2): best response to those matched with; uniform error (best response with probability 1 − ε, random otherwise); log-linear response; when matches are rare (duelling), a random sample from the history of the other population's actions with a trembled best response; recency by exponential discounting or a truncated memory.
- Three qualitative features [V] (same source, §4.4): persistence and tipping, "long periods in which people follow a norm ... interrupted by a burst of activity in which one norm is displaced by another (the punctuated equilibrium effect)"; the compression effect (less diverse choices than fundamentals warrant); local conformity / global diversity (non-interacting communities settle on different norms under the same influences).
- Illinois share contracts (Young and Burke 2001) [V] (same source, §7): Illinois Cooperative Extension Service survey (1995) of several thousand farms; over 98% of share contracts were 1/2–1/2, 2/5–3/5 or 1/3–2/3; the north uses 1/2–1/2, the south 1/3–2/3 or 2/5–3/5 (the boundary roughly that of the last glaciation); farms of equal soil quality in different regions operate under different shares; compression predicts an upward bias in tenants' net income on high-quality land, "confirmed by the Illinois data (Burke 2004)". Young notes identification from cross-sections is "extremely difficult" (Manski 1993; Brock and Durlauf 2001). The paper itself: Young, H.P. and Burke, M.A. (2001), "Competition and custom in economic contracts: a case study of Illinois agriculture", *AER* 91(3):559–573 [M].
- Footbinding (Mackie 1996) [V] (same source, §8.1): a norm that persisted about a thousand years "disappeared almost entirely within a single generation"; top-down edicts had failed; reformers organised "natural foot societies" whose families pledged not to bind daughters nor let sons marry bound women, shifting expectations of interacting families simultaneously; Mackie proposes this as a template for female genital cutting.
- Further norm literature listed by Young [S via reference list]: Sugden (1986) *The Economics of Rights, Cooperation and Welfare*; Skyrms (1996) *Evolution of the Social Contract*, (2004) *The Stag Hunt and the Evolution of Social Structure*; Bowles (2004) *Microeconomics: Behavior, Institutions, and Evolution*, Princeton UP; Belloc and Bowles (2013) on persistence of inferior cultural-institutional conventions, AER P&P 103; Bowles and Choi (2013), "Coevolution of farming and private property during the early Holocene", PNAS 110 (pages printed as 8830–8735 in Young's list, evidently a typo; [M] 8830–8835, DOI 10.1073/pnas.1212149110); Boyd and Richerson (2002), "Group beneficial norms can spread rapidly in a structured population", JTB 215:287–296; Hwang, Naidu, Bowles (2014) on social conflict and unequal conventions ([Young 2015 preprint](https://www.econ2.jhu.edu/people/young/SocNormsOCT1.pdf)).

**Cultural transmission**
- Cavalli-Sforza and Feldman (1981) mechanism, as stated in Bisin and Verdier's survey [V] ([NBER w16512](https://www.nber.org/papers/w16512)): dichotomous trait {a, b}, share q_i; direct vertical socialisation with probability d_i, otherwise oblique adoption from a random role model; P_ii = d_i + (1 − d_i)q_i, P_ij = (1 − d_i)(1 − q_i); continuous-time dynamics q̇_i = q_i(1 − q_i)(d_i − d_j) (eq. 3), "a simple version of the replicator dynamics", logistic; with exogenous d_i > d_j the trait i fixes from any q_0 ∈ (0, 1]; with d_i = d_j nothing moves. Boyd and Richerson (1985) allow exogenous frequency-dependent d_i = d(q_i).
- Bisin and Verdier (2001), "The economics of cultural transmission and the dynamics of preferences", *JET* 97(2):298–319, DOI 10.1006/jeth.2000.2678 [S] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/jetheo/v97y2001i2p298-319.html)). Model as stated in the survey [V] ([NBER w16512](https://www.nber.org/papers/w16512)): "imperfect empathy" V^{ij} = u^i(x^j) with x^j = argmax u^j, so V^{ii} ≥ V^{ij}; parents choose d_i to maximise −C(d_i) + P^{ii}V^{ii} + P^{ij}V^{ij}; with quadratic cost C(d) = d²/2, d_i = (1 − q_i)ΔV^i where ΔV^i = V^{ii} − V^{ij} is "cultural intolerance" (eq. 2); then the stationary states are 0, 1 and an interior q*, and "the culturally heterogeneous stationary state is globally stable" on (0, 1). Cultural substitution (d_i continuous, strictly decreasing in q_i, d_i(1, ·) = 0) makes homogeneous states unstable; strong cultural complementarity can drive the population to homogeneity.
- The survey itself: Bisin, A. and Verdier, T. (2010), "The economics of cultural transmission and socialization", NBER Working Paper 16512 (published in the *Handbook of Social Economics*, 2011) [V] ([NBER](https://www.nber.org/papers/w16512)).
- Montgomery (2010), "Intergenerational cultural transmission as an evolutionary game", *AEJ: Micro* 2:115–136, connects the two literatures [S via Sandholm's reference list].

**Co-evolution of institutions and behaviour**
- Bowles, S., Choi, J.-K., Hopfensitz, A. (2003), "The co-evolution of individual behaviors and social institutions", *Journal of Theoretical Biology* 223:135–147, DOI 10.1016/S0022-5193(03)00060-2: agent-based simulations of a deme-structured population; group institutions spread culturally, individual behaviours are inherited; intergroup conflict can explain altruism toward unrelated group members and institutions such as food sharing, when group-beneficial behaviours impose costs on outsiders and institutions reduce their cost to individuals [S] ([ADS](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2003JThBi.223..135B/abstract)).
- Related [M]: Choi, J.-K. and Bowles, S. (2007), "The coevolution of parochial altruism and war", *Science* 318:636–640, DOI 10.1126/science.1144237; Bowles and Gintis (2011), *A Cooperative Species*, Princeton UP; Gintis (2007), "The evolution of private property", *JEBO* 64:1–16 (property as a convention via a bourgeois strategy, after Maynard Smith's Hawk–Dove–Bourgeois); Boyd and Richerson (1985), *Culture and the Evolutionary Process*, Chicago UP.

**Institutions as equilibria (Aoki) and the commons (Ostrom)**
- Aoki (2001), *Toward a Comparative Institutional Analysis*, MIT Press: a section titled "Aspects of Institutions: Shared Beliefs, Summary Representations of Equilibrium, and Endogenous Rules of the Game"; institutions as shared beliefs about how the game is played, self-enforcing, a "summary representation" of an equilibrium that may or may not match formal law; chapter 7 elaborates this rules-in-equilibrium view [S] ([RIETI slides](https://www.rieti.go.jp/en/events/03010801/pdf/Aoki_p.pdf); [Cambridge JIE 2025 commentary](https://www.cambridge.org/core/product/B8153640E3B8C0568B71C48E8A42CF01/core-reader)). A 2025 commentary places Aoki within equilibrium theories of institutional change, needing comparative and historical analysis to explain which equilibrium is selected [S] (same Cambridge source).
- Ostrom (1990), *Governing the Commons*, Cambridge UP (design principles for long-enduring common-pool resource institutions), and Ostrom (2005), *Understanding Institutional Diversity*, Princeton UP (the IAD framework and the "grammar of institutions": attributes, deontic, aim, conditions, or else) [M]; not verified in this session.

#### Inferences
- Bisin–Verdier with quadratic cost gives a closed form: q̇ = q(1 − q)[(1 − q)ΔV^a − qΔV^b], so q* = ΔV^a/(ΔV^a + ΔV^b), with derivative at q* equal to −q*(1 − q*)(ΔV^a + ΔV^b) < 0 (derived from the survey's equations 2–3; not printed in the extract read).
- Young's Gibbs result makes norm dynamics on networks exactly checkable for small networks: enumerate the 2^n action profiles, compute ρ*, and compare simulated long-run frequencies with e^{βρ*}.
- The three signatures (punctuated equilibrium, compression, local conformity / global diversity) are testable outputs of a simulation on a structured population (regions as weakly linked graphs), and suit the urban and social domains as empirical targets.
- Aoki's "summary representation of an equilibrium" and Ostrom's rule grammar suggest representing an institution as a declared object with two faces: a rule text (kybernesis-style declared data, or the law domain's norms) and the equilibrium beliefs that make it self-enforcing; kategoria could check the mapping between them.

#### Gaps
- No formal model of institutional change in Ostrom's or Aoki's sense was read in full; the summaries above are secondary.
- Boyd–Richerson conformist transmission (frequency-dependent bias) formulas were not verified.
- Quantitative evidence on how often observed norms coincide with the stochastically stable state (beyond the cases Young reports) was not found.

### 5. Evolutionary Approaches to Economic Policy

#### Takeaway
Neo-Schumpeterian economics recasts policy as acting on variety, selection and retention in an "experimental economy" (Metcalfe 1994, 1995); later strands add directionality (Mazzucato's missions; Schot and Steinmueller's transformative frame), discovery (Hausmann and Rodrik's self-discovery; Rodrik's industrial policy as a process), and recursive revision of provisional frameworks (Sabel and Zeitlin's experimentalist governance). Formal work that treats policy itself as an evolving object exists mainly in political science (learning and diffusion, policy search on a Brownian landscape) and in adaptive management; agent-based macroeconomics uses evolutionary learning chiefly for private agents. Evidence on outcomes is thin: OECD stocktakes (2024–2025) find conventional evaluation unsuited to missions and report countries downsizing or abandoning them; adaptive management is rarely implemented as designed; the newer causal literature on industrial policy is more favourable than the older correlational one.

#### Cited Findings

**Variety, selection, adaptive policy**
- Metcalfe, J.S. (1994), "Evolutionary economics and technology policy", *Economic Journal* 104(425):931–944, DOI 10.2307/2234988: technology policy should be designed for an "experimental economy" in which market selection and variety reinforce each other [S] ([catalogue record](https://bvbvf1.bib-bvb.de/vufind/PrimoRecord/cdi_proquest_miscellaneous_38653031); [EconBiz](https://econbiz.de/Record/evolutionary-economics-and-technology-policy-metcalfe-john/10001332204)).
- Metcalfe, J.S. (1995), "Technology systems and technology policy in an evolutionary framework", *Cambridge Journal of Economics* 19(1):25–46 [S] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/oup/cambje/v19y1995i1p25-46.html)); DOI 10.1093/oxfordjournals.cje.a035307 [M]. [M]: Metcalfe contrasts the "market failure" policymaker (optimising against equilibrium benchmarks) with an "adaptive policymaker" who is uncertain, learns, and supports the generation of variety and the operation of selection, drawing on Fisher's principle that the rate of change of mean fitness equals the variance of fitness in the population (his "replicator" models of competition).
- [M] Nelson and Winter (1982), *An Evolutionary Theory of Economic Change*, Belknap: the search, routines and selection model from which these policy views descend.

**Missions and transformative innovation policy**
- Mazzucato, M. (2018), "Mission-oriented innovation policies: challenges and opportunities", *Industrial and Corporate Change* 27(5):803–815; reviews mission-oriented programmes, the features offering lessons, and how to choose and implement missions [S] ([EconPapers](https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:oup:indcch:v:27:y:2018:i:5:p:803-815.); [UCL](https://www.ucl.ac.uk/bartlett/public-purpose/publications/2017/dec/mission-oriented-innovation-policy-challenges-and-opportunities)); DOI 10.1093/icc/dty034 [M, unconfirmed by the search]; earlier IIPP Working Paper 2017-01 [S].
- Schot, J. and Steinmueller, W.E. (2018), "Three frames for innovation policy: R&D, systems of innovation and transformative change", *Research Policy* 47(9):1554–1567, DOI 10.1016/j.respol.2018.08.011 (open access): frame 1, post-1945 support for science and R&D; frame 2, from the 1980s, competitiveness and national innovation systems; frame 3, societal and environmental challenges (the SDGs) calling for transformative change; all three remain relevant but transformative options should be a priority [S] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/respol/v47y2018i9p1554-1567.html); [open copy](https://homepages.tuni.fi/markku.sotarauta/hal_kajo_312/three_frames.pdf)). A response by Fagerberg in the same issue (47(9):1568–1576) argues that existing innovation-studies knowledge is more useful than Schot and Steinmueller allow [S] (same search).

**Discovery and experimentalism**
- Hausmann, R. and Rodrik, D. (2003), "Economic development as self-discovery", *Journal of Development Economics* 72(2):603–633: laissez-faire gives too little investment and entrepreneurship ex ante (discovery of what a country can produce is a non-appropriable public good) and too much diversification ex post [S] ([EconPapers](https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:eee:deveco:v:72:y:2003:i:2:p:603-633); [NBER w8952](https://www.nber.org/papers/w8952), DOI 10.3386/w8952); journal DOI 10.1016/S0304-3878(03)00124-X [inferred from the PII, M].
- Rodrik (2004), "Industrial policy for the twenty-first century", KSG Faculty Research Working Paper RWP04-047 / CEPR DP 4767 [M]: industrial policy as a process of strategic collaboration between government and firms to discover where obstacles lie, with "carrots and sticks", sunset clauses and the expectation that some projects fail [M]; not verified.
- Juhász, R., Lane, N., Rodrik, D. (2024), "The new economics of industrial policy", *Annual Review of Economics* 16:213–242, DOI 10.1146/annurev-economics-081023-024638: the new empirical work, attentive to measurement, causal inference and economic structure, is "a significant improvement over the earlier generation of empirical work, which was largely correlational", asks whether a policy elicited the desired behavioural response in a given setting, and reads more favourably for industrial policy [S] ([EconPapers](https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:anr:reveco:v:16:y:2024:p:213-242); [NBER w31538](https://nber.org/papers/w31538)).
- Sabel, C.F. and Zeitlin, J. (2012), "Experimentalist governance", in Levi-Faur (ed.), *Oxford Handbook of Governance* (pages 169–183 in one record): governance that "sets provisional frameworks for action and revises them in light of recursive review of implementation efforts", with its accountability, monitoring and compliance mechanisms analysed [S] ([UvA record](https://dare.uva.nl/id/8bafd6ac-ef3f-4d2c-875f-3a72e0274393)). [M]: the architecture is a four-step cycle: framework goals and metrics set jointly; lower-level units given discretion to pursue them; units report performance and take part in peer review against each other; goals, metrics and procedures revised in light of the review.

**Policy itself as an evolving object (formal work)**
- Volden, C., Ting, M.M., Carpenter, D.P. (2008), "A formal model of learning and policy diffusion", *APSR* 102(3):319–332: two models, governments learning only from their own experiments and governments learning from one another's; "much accepted scholarly evidence of policy diffusion could simply have arisen through independent actions by governments that only learn from their own experiences"; the models' differences suggest tests that separate learning-based diffusion from independent adoption [S] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/cup/apsrev/v102y2008i03p319-332_08.html); [Cambridge](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/american-political-science-review/article/formal-model-of-learning-and-policy-diffusion/C5547E66DBC3D19C91631FA65B0306C2)); DOI 10.1017/S0003055408080222 [M].
- Callander, S. (2011), "Searching for good policies", *APSR* 105(4):643–662: the mapping from policies to outcomes is the realised path of a Brownian motion whose drift and variance are known but whose path is not; experimentation continues until an outcome near the median voter's ideal is found, "often but not always", and policymaking "can get stuck at outcomes that are arbitrarily bad"; uncertainty over voter preferences improves policymaking over time [S] ([IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/cup/apsrev/v105y2011i04p643-662_00.html); [working paper](https://www.wallis.rochester.edu/assets/pdf/conference15/Callander_Searching_Wallis2008.pdf)). Companion: Callander (2011), "Searching and learning by trial and error", *AER* 101(6):2277–2308, same Brownian representation applied to product development [S] ([EconPapers](https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:aea:aecrev:v:101:y:2011:i:6:p:2277-2308)).
- Adaptive management: Westgate, M.J., Likens, G.E., Lindenmayer, D.B. (2013), "Adaptive management of biological systems: a review", *Biological Conservation* 158:128–139, DOI 10.1016/j.biocon.2012.08.016: of about 1,336 articles found, only 61 (< 5%) explicitly claimed to enact adaptive management, describing 54 projects, of which only 13 were supported by published monitoring data, and these varied enormously in how well they applied monitoring and the comparison of alternative actions [S] ([ANU](https://researchportalplus.anu.edu.au/en/publications/adaptive-management-of-biological-systems-a-review/)). [M]: Holling (1978) and Walters (1986) define active adaptive management as treating management options as experiments chosen partly for what they teach, the dual-control problem.
- Agent-based macroeconomics: genetic-algorithm learning is used for private agents' forecasting heuristics, e.g. Hommes, Makarewicz, Massaro, Smits (2017), "Genetic algorithm learning in a New Keynesian macroeconomic setup", *Journal of Evolutionary Economics* [S] ([author PDF](https://air.unimi.it/retrieve/dfa8b9a5-b920-748b-e053-3a05fe0a3a96/Hommes2017_Article_GeneticAlgorithmLearningInANew.pdf)); a search for ABMs in which the policymaker's rule itself evolves returned no direct match; Fagiolo and Roventini describe ABMs as a "laboratory" for policy exercises and design [S] (same search). [M]: Arifovic (1994, JEDC) introduced GA learning in the cobweb model; Dosi, Fagiolo, Roventini (2010), "Schumpeter meeting Keynes", JEDC 34:1748–1767, evaluate fixed policy rules in an evolutionary ABM.
- [M] Lindblom (1959), "The science of muddling through", *Public Administration Review* 19:79–88 (incrementalism as successive limited comparison); Heilmann (2008), "Policy experimentation in China's economic rise", *Studies in Comparative International Development* 43:1–26 (experimentation under hierarchy, local pilots scaled up); Shipan and Volden (2008), "The mechanisms of policy diffusion", *AJPS* 52:840–857 (learning, competition, imitation, coercion). Not verified in this session.

**Evidence on outcomes, to 2026**
- OECD, *Forging New Frontiers in Mission-Oriented Innovation Policies* (report dated December 2025 in its PDF path, built around the 2024 Vienna Mission Forward Conference): conventional monitoring and evaluation are "ill-suited" for missions, which need formative and developmental evaluation; it proposes assessing "second-order additionality" (systemic effects beyond those of the steered instruments); the conclusion notes a shift toward competitiveness and strategic autonomy and that several countries have downsized, restructured or abandoned mission-oriented initiatives [S] ([OECD](https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/forging-new-frontiers-in-mission-oriented-innovation-policies_d13d0142-en.html); the conclusion page returned HTTP 403 when fetched, so the country names were not read).
- OECD, *Mission-Oriented Innovation Policies for Net Zero* (November 2024): 101 net-zero missions and 17 in-depth case studies; MOIPs are a marked improvement over traditional STI policy mixes in setting ambitious goals; selection bias makes comparisons of beneficiaries with non-beneficiaries "hardly meaningful" as counterfactuals [S] ([OECD PDF](https://www.oecd.org/content/dam/oecd/en/publications/reports/2024/11/mission-oriented-innovation-policies-for-net-zero_842360c5/5efdbc5c-en.pdf)).
- An OECD STI working paper on monitoring and evaluation of MOIPs offers a self-assessment framework of "MOIP design principles", not an outcome scorecard [S] ([ResearchGate listing](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/385774570_Monitoring_and_evaluation_of_mission-oriented_innovation_policies_From_theory_to_practice_OECD_Science_Technology_and_Industry_Working_Papers)). Larrue (2021), "The design and implementation of mission-oriented innovation policies", OECD STI Policy Paper 100 [S title; DOI 10.1787/3f6c76a4-en M].

#### Inferences
- The policy literature supplies the vocabulary of variation (portfolios, pilots, local discretion), selection (evaluation, peer review, budgets) and retention (scaling, framework revision), but almost no formal law of motion; the closest formal objects are bandit/experimentation models (Callander's Brownian landscape; Volden–Ting–Carpenter's own vs. social learning) and adaptive management's dual control.
- The evidence problem is structural: missions and experimentalist programmes are chosen non-randomly and aim at systemic effects, so the OECD's "second-order additionality" is a counterfactual over policy configurations, which an evolutionary model could make explicit by simulating the same population of instruments with and without coordination.
- Volden et al.'s identification point (diffusion indistinguishable from independent learning in adoption data) parallels Salmon's point for learning rules: models of policy evolution should come with a statement of what adoption records can and cannot identify.

#### Gaps
- The EU's own assessment of Horizon Europe missions (2023) and later evaluations were not read; no outcome figures for them are reported.
- No systematic evidence on "policy labs" (public sector innovation labs) outcomes was found in this session.
- No agent-based model in which the policymaker's rule evolves by selection was verified; this may exist under "learning central bank" or "adaptive Taylor rule" searches not run here.
- Rodrik (2004), Metcalfe's "adaptive policymaker" passage, Sabel–Zeitlin's four-step architecture, Heilmann (2008) and the Ostrom/Aoki formal content are [M] or [S].

### 6. What an Implementation Would Need

#### Takeaway
A sub-domain (or a foundation module, since the dynamics name no object in nature) needs: revision protocols as first-class functions with mean-field and finite-N Markov realisations; the five canonical dynamics plus imitative, excess-payoff, pairwise-comparison and perturbed-best-response families; PC and NS as checkable laws; potential and contractive game tests with their Lyapunov functions; exact stationary distributions where reversibility holds and resistance-tree computation elsewhere; EWA-family learning; cultural transmission; and a representation of policy as a population of instruments under declared variation, selection and retention operators whose fitness comes from the existing policy domain's evidence and value of information.

#### Cited Findings
- The pieces with exact formulas and theorems are those listed above: Table 13.1 protocols and dynamics, Table 13.3 properties, Theorems 13.6–13.7 (potential games), 13.9 and Table 13.4 (contractive games), RPS/Mismatching Pennies/feeble-twin examples ([Sandholm 2015](https://users.ssc.wisc.edu/~whs/teaching/805/handbook.pdf)) [V]; Theorem 5.3 product formula, Theorem 7.7 limits, Corollaries 7.11–7.12 risk dominance ([Sandholm AMS](https://users.ssc.wisc.edu/~whs/research/ams.pdf)) [V]; Gibbs distribution in potential games ([Young 2015](https://www.econ2.jhu.edu/people/young/SocNormsOCT1.pdf)) [V]; Bisin–Verdier dynamics ([NBER w16512](https://www.nber.org/papers/w16512)) [V].

#### Inferences

**Dynamics and protocols worth implementing (in order)**
1. A `protocol` interface ρ(π, x) → nonnegative n×n matrix, with multi-population support; the mean dynamic ẋ_i = Σ_j x_j ρ_ji − x_i Σ_j ρ_ij; a finite-N continuous-time Markov jump process (agents revise at Poisson rate R) built from the same protocol; and a differential-inclusion integrator for best response (or its logit approximation with small η).
2. Protocols: pairwise proportional imitation (replicator), Maynard Smith replicator, imitative logit, best response, logit (η), BNN, Smith, projection, BRM(ε), probit; the general imitative (r_ij), excess-payoff (τ) and pairwise-comparison families as parametrised classes; hybrids.
3. Game classes: normal-form matrices, population games F(x), congestion games (potential), random contractive games (DF negative semidefinite on TX), supermodular games.
4. Stochastic: exact stationary distribution by linear algebra for small state spaces; birth–death formula for two strategies; Gibbs in potential games; resistance-tree stochastic potential via minimum-weight arborescences over recurrent classes; Ellison radius/coradius; waiting-time estimation by simulation.
5. Learning: EWA with its special cases; Roth–Erev with forgetting and experimentation; stochastic fictitious play; regret matching (Hart–Mas-Colell) [M]; fitting with a parameter-recovery report.
6. Cultural transmission: Cavalli-Sforza–Feldman, Boyd–Richerson frequency dependence, Bisin–Verdier endogenous socialisation; norm dynamics on networks with log-linear response (reusing nomos's agents and Kirman herding, tyche's networks).

**Checks with known answers**
- Table 13.1 identities: each protocol's mean dynamic equals the closed form (symbolic or numerical, random F and x).
- PC holds for replicator, BR, BNN, Smith (V′F > 0 off rest points, random games); NS fails for the replicator (pure states are rest points that need not be Nash) and holds for BNN and Smith.
- Standard RPS (w = l): x₁x₂x₃ is conserved along the replicator (derived: d/dt Σ log x_i = Σ_i F_i − 3F̄ = 0 since x′Ax = 0 for antisymmetric A and A's column sums vanish when w = l); equivalently H_{x*} is constant, consistent with the closed orbits in the chapter. The other four dynamics converge to (1/3, 1/3, 1/3).
- Good RPS: all five converge to x*, with the Table 13.4 Lyapunov functions decreasing. Bad RPS (l = 2, w = 1): replicator approaches the boundary heteroclinic cycle (min_i x_i → 0 along time), others an interior limit cycle.
- Feeble twin (d = 0.1, Smith): the dominated strategy's share stays above 10% recurrently (the chapter's thresholds 0.31, 0.47, 0.66 for 10%, 5%, 1%).
- Mismatching Pennies: replicator time averages approach the six-sided Shapley polygon of the best response dynamic.
- Potential games: the potential increases along every PC dynamic; asymptotically stable states equal isolated local maximisers (Theorem 13.7).
- Hawk–Dove (C > V): convergence to Hawk share V/C [M].
- Kurtz: finite-N sample paths within ε of the ODE on [0, T] with probability → 1; Benaïm–Weibull's exponential rate as a statistical check.
- Two-strategy coordination: Theorem 5.3's product equals the stationary vector of the generator; (η/N) log μ approaches ΔI; the risk-dominant state is selected (strategy 1 if x* < ½); the N = 100, ε = 10⁻⁵ vs 10⁻⁷ contrast reproduces.
- Logit in potential games: stationary distribution ∝ exp(η^{-1} × potential) on the finite grid (Blume; Young's Theorem 1), checked exactly for small N or small networks.
- Fictitious play: convergence in zero-sum and 2×2 games [M: Robinson 1951; Miyasawa 1961] and the Shapley (1964) cycle.
- Börgers–Sarin: Cross reinforcement with small step approaches the replicator path.
- EWA: δ = 0, ρ = 0 reproduces cumulative reinforcement; δ = 1, ρ = φ reproduces weighted fictitious play (exact equality of attractions up to normalisation) [M formulas].
- Bisin–Verdier: q* = ΔV^a/(ΔV^a + ΔV^b), globally attracting on (0, 1); with exogenous d_i > d_j, fixation of trait i.

**Policy as evolution, formally**
- State: a population (or portfolio) of policy instruments or programme variants k ∈ K with shares (budget weights) x_k, each carrying a theory of change and a posterior over its effect (from the policy domain's `evidence`, `transfer`, `adoption`).
- Fitness: expected net value under the posterior, optionally plus the value of information of continuing to fund it (the policy domain's EVPI/EVSI), so that selection rewards both performance and learning; this is the dual-control idea of active adaptive management in the language of revision protocols.
- Variation: new variants proposed (local discretion in experimentalist governance; Rodrik's discovery; Callander's search over a Brownian landscape with known drift and variance), as mutation operators with declared rates; directionality (missions) as a constraint set on admissible variants.
- Selection: a revision protocol over budget shares (replicator = proportional reallocation to above-average programmes; logit = soft reallocation with temperature as political inertia; Smith = pairwise comparisons by programme managers; BRM = rare random reallocation), so that PC, NS and the stability theorems transfer and say when a portfolio settles, cycles, or keeps a dominated programme alive.
- Retention and revision: the experimentalist cycle (framework, discretion, report and peer review, revision) as a loop in kybernesis's architecture, each turn sealed in archeion's journal; diffusion among jurisdictions as a network process with own vs. social learning (Volden–Ting–Carpenter), where adoption records alone may not identify which.
- Evaluation: OECD's "second-order additionality" as the difference between the portfolio's outcome under a coordinated protocol and under independent protocols for the same instruments, computed by simulation.

#### Gaps
- No verified source was found that couples a revision-protocol dynamic over policy portfolios with value-of-information fitness; this is a design proposal, not a reported literature result.
- Whether the user's existing `nomos::games`, `heuresis::evolution` and `kinesis` already provide the integrator, the arborescence solver or the stationary-distribution solver was not checked in this session.

## Part IV. Evolutionary Economic Geography, Economic Complexity and the Evolution of Technologies

*Remark.* Part IV's scope and conventions. Written for the designer of an evolutionary-economics sub-domain. Markers: **[V]** verified at a source read in this research (full text or abstract page, named); **[S]** secondary (a search-result summary, a catalogue record, or a restatement in another paper); **[M]** from memory, unverified in this research. DOIs marked [M] were not resolved and should be checked before citation. The research was limited to sixteen tool calls; most journal pages were paywalled, so arXiv and ar5iv versions were read where possible. No Japanese city is used as a worked case anywhere in this Part.

Two identities used throughout, both verified in the sources read: a rank-size law size(r) ∝ r^(−a) corresponds to a frequency density P(k) ∝ k^(−(1+1/a)) (Tria et al. state P(n) ~ n^(−(1+ν/ρ)) with rank law f(R) ~ R^(−ρ/ν), [V] [ar5iv 1310.1953](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1310.1953)); and Simon's process gives rank exponent a = 1 − α, with α the probability of a new type (Youn et al. use γ = 1 − α, [V] [ar5iv 1405.3202](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1405.3202)).

### 1. Evolutionary Economic Geography: Branching, Variety, Path Dependence and Resilience

#### Takeaway
Evolutionary economic geography (EEG) has one canonical formal model, Frenken and Boschma's (2007) branching process, a Simon-type urn in which product innovations open growth opportunities for existing or new firms in existing or new cities, so that firm-size and city-size (Zipf) distributions emerge from one process; the rest of the field (related/unrelated variety, path dependence, resilience) is mainly measurement plus conceptual frameworks, with formal content borrowed from entropy decomposition (Theil), Pólya/Arthur urns, Klepper's spinoff model, NK landscapes and shift-share.

#### Cited Findings

**Founding programme**
- Boschma and Frenken (2006), "Why is economic geography not an evolutionary science? Towards an evolutionary economic geography", J. Econ. Geogr. 6(3):273–302, set EEG against neoclassical and institutional economic geography: the unit is the firm and its routines, and regional development is explained by variety, selection and retention of routines in space. [M] [doi:10.1093/jeg/lbi022](https://doi.org/10.1093/jeg/lbi022)
- Boschma and Frenken (2011), "The emerging empirics of evolutionary economic geography", J. Econ. Geogr. 11(2):295–307, survey the empirical turn: spinoffs, labour mobility, networks and relatedness as the vehicles of routine inheritance. [M] [doi:10.1093/jeg/lbq053](https://doi.org/10.1093/jeg/lbq053)
- Boschma (2005), "Proximity and innovation: a critical assessment", Regional Studies 39(1):61–74: five proximities (cognitive, organisational, social, institutional, geographical), with the "proximity paradox" that too much proximity causes lock-in. [M] [doi:10.1080/0034340052000320887](https://doi.org/10.1080/0034340052000320887)

**The branching model (Frenken and Boschma 2007)**
- Published as Boschma and Frenken / Frenken and Boschma (2007), "A theoretical framework for evolutionary economic geography: industrial dynamics and urban growth as a branching process", J. Econ. Geogr. 7(5):635–649; working paper PEEG 0701 (Utrecht). [S] [IDEAS record](https://ideas.repec.org/a/oup/jecgeo/v7y2007i5p635-649.html); DOI [M] [doi:10.1093/jeg/lbm018](https://doi.org/10.1093/jeg/lbm018)
- Abstract-level content: economic development is an evolutionary branching process of product innovations; each product innovation provides a growth opportunity for an existing firm or a new firm, and for an existing city or a new city; firm-size and city-size distributions are two aggregates of a single process; gains from variety at firm level (economies of scope) and urban level (Jacobs externalities) are the central feedback producing path dependence; gains from size are bounded by rising wages. Zipf is among the paper's keywords. [S] [IDEAS](https://ideas.repec.org/a/oup/jecgeo/v7y2007i5p635-649.html), [CORE](https://core.ac.uk/works/2955061)
- A 2009 Frenken chapter states that Simon's model of the rank-size rule "is still regarded as one of the canonical models of urban size distribution", which is the lineage the branching model extends. [S] [Springer chapter](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-642-01554-0_10)
- Mechanism as I recall it (full text not read): with a small probability a new product appears in a new firm in a new city; otherwise a new product is assigned to an existing firm/city with probability proportional to its current number of products (diversification through routine replication, spinoffs and labour mobility), which is Simon's process at two levels and yields Zipf's law in the limit of a small entry probability. [M] (equations unverified; see Gaps)

**Related and unrelated variety (Frenken, Van Oort and Verburg 2007)**
- "Related variety, unrelated variety and regional economic growth", Regional Studies 41(5):685–697. [M] [doi:10.1080/00343400601120296](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343400601120296)
- Measure (Theil's entropy decomposition; standard formulation, [M]): with p_i the employment share of fine industry i (5-digit), grouped into coarse sectors S_g (2-digit) with P_g = Σ_{i∈S_g} p_i:
  - total entropy H = Σ_i p_i log₂(1/p_i);
  - unrelated variety UV = Σ_g P_g log₂(1/P_g) (entropy at the 2-digit level);
  - related variety RV = Σ_g P_g H_g, with H_g = Σ_{i∈S_g} (p_i/P_g) log₂(P_g/p_i);
  - identity H = UV + RV (chain rule of entropy), exact for any data. [M] [doi:10.1080/00343400601120296](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343400601120296)
- Findings for Dutch regions (40 COROP regions, about 1996–2002, as I recall): related variety raises employment growth (Jacobs externalities among related sectors); unrelated variety dampens growth of unemployment (a portfolio effect against sector-specific shocks); productivity growth is not explained by variety. [M] [doi:10.1080/00343400601120296](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343400601120296)
- Nomaler and Verspagen (2023) find unrelated variety significant alongside related variety in regressions predicting gains or losses of specialisation, the influence of related variety growing with a country's level of diversification ("only countries that are already diversified show a strong influence of related variety"). [V] [arXiv:2303.14458](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.14458)

**Path dependence and lock-in**
- Arthur (1989), "Competing technologies, increasing returns, and lock-in by historical events", Economic Journal 99(394):116–131: adopters arrive sequentially; with increasing returns, the market share converges almost surely to 0 or 1, which one being decided by early random arrivals (generalised Pólya urns, Arthur, Ermoliev and Kaniovski 1987); with constant returns shares are predictable; with diminishing returns they converge to an interior point. [M] [doi:10.2307/2234208](https://doi.org/10.2307/2234208)
- David (1985), "Clio and the economics of QWERTY", AER 75(2):332–337: technical interrelatedness, scale economies and quasi-irreversibility of investment as the conditions of lock-in. [M] (no DOI; JSTOR 1805621)
- Martin and Sunley (2006), "Path dependence and regional economic evolution", J. Econ. Geogr. 6(4):395–437: critique of the "canonical" lock-in model for regions (place-dependence, the problem of explaining path creation and de-locking), proposing mechanisms of layering, conversion and recombination. [M] [doi:10.1093/jeg/lbl012](https://doi.org/10.1093/jeg/lbl012)
- Martin (2010), "Rethinking regional path dependence: beyond lock-in to evolution", Economic Geography 86(1):1–27: an alternative "path-as-process" model in which paths evolve by incremental change, without an equilibrium. [M] [doi:10.1111/j.1944-8287.2009.01056.x](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1944-8287.2009.01056.x)
- Klepper (2007), "Disagreements, spinoffs, and the evolution of Detroit as the capital of the U.S. automobile industry", Management Science 53(4):616–631: a heredity model in which spinoffs inherit their parents' capabilities and locate near them, generating clusters without agglomeration externalities. [M] [doi:10.1287/mnsc.1060.0683](https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.1060.0683)
- Frenken (2006), "Technological innovation and complexity theory", Econ. Innov. New Techn. 15(2):137–155: NK fitness landscapes as models of technological search, local optima as lock-in. [M] [doi:10.1080/10438590500141453](https://doi.org/10.1080/10438590500141453)
- Neffke, Henning and Boschma (2011), "How do regions diversify over time? Industry relatedness and the development of new growth paths in regions", Economic Geography 87(3):237–265: Swedish regions 1969–2002; industries technologically related (revealed by product co-occurrence in plants) to a region's portfolio are more likely to enter it and less likely to exit. [M] [doi:10.1111/j.1944-8287.2011.01121.x](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1944-8287.2011.01121.x)
- Neffke, Hartog, Boschma and Henning (2018), "Agents of structural change: the role of firms and entrepreneurs in regional diversification", Economic Geography 94(1):23–48: new establishments, especially those from outside the region, introduce more unrelated diversification than incumbents. [M] [doi:10.1080/00130095.2017.1391691](https://doi.org/10.1080/00130095.2017.1391691)

**Adaptive cycle and regional resilience**
- Simmie and Martin (2010), "The economic resilience of regions: towards an evolutionary approach", Cambridge J. Regions, Econ. Soc. 3(1):27–43: Holling's adaptive cycle (exploitation r, conservation K, release Ω, reorganisation α; panarchy of nested cycles) applied to Cambridge and Swansea. [M] [doi:10.1093/cjres/rsp029](https://doi.org/10.1093/cjres/rsp029)
- Martin (2012), "Regional economic resilience, hysteresis and recessionary shocks", J. Econ. Geogr. 12(1):1–32: four dimensions (resistance, recovery, re-orientation, renewal) and the distinction between engineering, ecological and adaptive resilience; a resistance index comparing a region's employment fall with the nation's. [M] [doi:10.1093/jeg/lbr019](https://doi.org/10.1093/jeg/lbr019)
- Martin, Sunley, Gardiner and Tyler (2016), "How regions react to recessions: resilience and the role of economic structure", Regional Studies 50(4):561–585: sensitivity index β_r = (ΔE_r/E_r)/(ΔE_N/E_N) and a shift-share split of resistance into a structural (industry-mix) and a competitive (region-specific) component, for UK regions across four recessions. [M] [doi:10.1080/00343404.2015.1136410](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343404.2015.1136410)
- Martin and Sunley (2015), "On the notion of regional economic resilience: conceptualization and explanation", J. Econ. Geogr. 15(1):1–42. [M] [doi:10.1093/jeg/lbu015](https://doi.org/10.1093/jeg/lbu015)
- Boschma (2015), "Towards an evolutionary perspective on regional resilience", Regional Studies 49(5):733–751: resilience as the capacity to develop new growth paths, linked to related and unrelated variety. [M] [doi:10.1080/00343404.2014.959481](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343404.2014.959481)
- Hassink (2010), "Regional resilience: a promising concept to explain differences in regional economic adaptability?", CJRES 3(1):45–58: critique of the equilibrium bias of the resilience notion. [M] [doi:10.1093/cjres/rsp033](https://doi.org/10.1093/cjres/rsp033)

#### Inferences
- The only closed-form generative content in EEG proper is Simon-type: the branching model inherits Simon's exponents, so an implementation can test it against the Simon closed forms of §5 (a = 1 − α for ranks).
- RV/UV is a two-level case of a hierarchical Shannon decomposition; the infrastructure's tyche `classes` (Jost's partition) and nomos `measurement` (concentration, specialisation and their decompositions) are the natural homes, and the identity H = UV + RV is a free check.
- "Resilience" as used in EEG has no dynamical model; its indices are shift-share arithmetic. The infrastructure's kinesis `transitions` (detectors of critical transitions with powered verdicts) is a stronger reading of "ecological resilience" than anything in the EEG literature I found.

#### Gaps
- The equations of Frenken and Boschma (2007) and their exact derivation of Zipf were not read (paywalled); the mechanism above is from memory.
- I did not verify the Dutch findings of Frenken, Van Oort and Verburg (2007) at the source, nor the sample years.
- No formal (equation-level) model of the adaptive cycle for regions was found; whether any EEG paper fits an adaptive-cycle dynamical system to regional data is unknown to me.

### 2. Relatedness and Diversification: the Product Space, Density, the Principle of Relatedness and Skill Relatedness

#### Takeaway
Relatedness is measured from co-occurrence (products co-exported, industries co-located or co-produced in plants, patent classes co-assigned) or from flows (job switchers between industries); a region's "density" around an activity predicts entry and (inversely) exit at every scale studied, which Hidalgo et al. (2018) call the principle of relatedness; out-of-sample work (Li and Neffke 2024; machine-learning entry prediction from the Pietronero group) shows relatedness predicts, with a trade-off between predictive skill and interpretability, while unrelated diversification is rare but real.

#### Cited Findings

**Product space (Hidalgo, Klinger, Barabási and Hausmann 2007)**
- "The product space conditions the development of nations", Science 317(5837):482–487. [M] [doi:10.1126/science.1144581](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1144581)
- Revealed comparative advantage (Balassa): RCA_cp = (x_cp / Σ_p x_cp) / (Σ_c x_cp / Σ_c Σ_p x_cp); M_cp = 1 if RCA_cp > 1 (Mealy et al.'s statement of the convention). [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)
- Proximity φ_ij = min{P(RCA_i ≥ 1 | RCA_j ≥ 1), P(RCA_j ≥ 1 | RCA_i ≥ 1)}, the minimum of the two conditional probabilities making it symmetric; about 775 SITC-4 products; a core-periphery network (machinery, chemicals in the core; primary goods at the periphery); drawn as a maximum spanning tree plus links φ ≥ 0.55; countries move to products close to their current specialisations. [M] [doi:10.1126/science.1144581](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1144581)
- Density of product j around country c: ω_cj = Σ_i M_ci φ_ij / Σ_i φ_ij; distance d_cj = 1 − ω_cj. [M] [doi:10.1126/science.1144581](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1144581)
- Derived Atlas measures (Hausmann, Hidalgo et al., *The Atlas of Economic Complexity*, MIT Press 2014): complexity outlook index COI_c = Σ_p (1 − d_cp)(1 − M_cp) PCI_p; complexity outlook gain COG_cp = Σ_{p'} [φ_{pp'} / Σ_{p''} φ_{p''p'}] (1 − M_cp') PCI_p' − (1 − d_cp) PCI_p. [M] (book ISBN 9780262525428)
- O'Clery, Yıldırım and Hausmann (2021), "Productive ecosystems and the arrow of development", Nature Communications 12:1479: a directed (asymmetric) product network giving a direction of development. [M] [doi:10.1038/s41467-021-21689-0](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-021-21689-0)

**Principle of relatedness**
- Hidalgo, Balland, Boschma, Delgado, Feldman, Frenken, Glaeser, He, Kogler, Morrison, Neffke, Rigby, Stern, Zheng and Zhu (2018), "The principle of relatedness", in *Unifying Themes in Complex Systems IX* (ICCS 2018), Springer Proceedings in Complexity, 451–457: the probability that a region enters an activity increases with the number of related activities present, and the probability of exit decreases, across countries, regions and cities, and across products, industries, technologies, occupations and research areas. [M] [doi:10.1007/978-3-319-96661-8_46](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-96661-8_46)
- Boschma, Balland and Kogler (2015), "Relatedness and technological change in cities: the rise and fall of technological knowledge in US metropolitan areas from 1981 to 2010", Industrial and Corporate Change 24(1):223–250: technological relatedness from patent co-classification; relatedness density RD_{i,c,t} = 100 × Σ_{j∈c, j≠i} φ_ij / Σ_{j≠i} φ_ij; entry of a technology into a metro rises with RD in linear-probability regressions with fixed effects. [M] [doi:10.1093/icc/dtu012](https://doi.org/10.1093/icc/dtu012)
- Rigby (2015), "Technological relatedness and knowledge space: entry and exit of US cities from patent classes", Regional Studies 49(11):1922–1937. [M] [doi:10.1080/00343404.2013.854878](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343404.2013.854878)
- Kogler, Rigby and Tucker (2013), "Mapping knowledge space and technological relatedness in US cities", European Planning Studies 21(9):1374–1391. [M] [doi:10.1080/09654313.2012.755832](https://doi.org/10.1080/09654313.2012.755832)
- Boschma (2017), "Relatedness as driver of regional diversification: a research agenda", Regional Studies 51(3):351–364. [M] [doi:10.1080/00343404.2016.1254767](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343404.2016.1254767)
- Bahar, Hausmann and Hidalgo (2014), "Neighbors and the evolution of the comparative advantage of nations", J. Int. Econ. 92(1):111–123: a country is more likely to gain RCA in a product its geographic neighbours already export. [M] [doi:10.1016/j.jinteco.2013.11.001](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jinteco.2013.11.001)
- An EC report on 292 NUTS-2 regions finds inter-regional linkages raise the probability of diversifying beyond the effect of regional capabilities (published as Balland and Boschma 2021, Regional Studies 55(6):1059–1070, [M] [doi:10.1080/00343404.2020.1861240](https://doi.org/10.1080/00343404.2020.1861240)). [S] [EC brochure](https://ec.europa.eu/regional_policy/sources/brochure/impact_ir_linkages_en.pdf)

**Skill relatedness**
- Neffke and Henning (2013), "Skill relatedness and firm diversification", Strategic Management Journal 34(3):297–316: relatedness from labour flows among Swedish industries, SR_ij = F_ij / (F_i· F_·j / F_··) (observed over expected job switches), often mapped to (−1, 1) by (SR − 1)/(SR + 1); firms diversify into skill-related industries. [M] [doi:10.1002/smj.2014](https://doi.org/10.1002/smj.2014)
- Neffke, Otto and Weyh (2017), "Inter-industry labor flows", J. Econ. Behav. Organ. 142:275–292: German labour-flow relatedness and its stability. [M] [doi:10.1016/j.jebo.2017.07.003](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jebo.2017.07.003)
- A 2025 study of 401 German NUTS-3 regions, 1993–2018, uses skill- and task-based relatedness and complexity and sequence analysis (optimal matching) to track the coherence of regional portfolios over time. [S] [Annals of Regional Science, doi:10.1007/s00168-025-01375-5](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00168-025-01375-5)

**Out-of-sample tests and their critique**
- Li and Neffke (2022 preprint; Research Policy 2024), "Evaluating the principle of relatedness: Estimation, drivers and implications for policy": relatedness matrices derived from co-occurrence in the portfolios of establishments, firms, cities and countries, each giving a different matrix; a structured search over tens of thousands of specifications judged by out-of-sample prediction; every matrix helps predict size and growth of local industries in US cities; a trade-off between predictive performance and interpretability; the authors argue for expanding policy frameworks that use relatedness analysis. [V abstract] [arXiv:2205.02942](https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.02942); journal DOI [S] [doi:10.1016/j.respol.2024.104952](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.respol.2024.104952)
- Tacchella, Zaccaria, Miccheli and Pietronero (2023), "Relatedness in the era of machine learning", Chaos, Solitons & Fractals 176:114071: supervised models (random forests, boosted trees) trained on export matrices outperform density at predicting which products a country will start exporting. [M] [doi:10.1016/j.chaos.2023.114071](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chaos.2023.114071)
- Albora, Pietronero, Tacchella and Zaccaria (2023), "Product progression: a machine learning approach to forecasting industrial upgrading", Scientific Reports 13:1481. [M] [doi:10.1038/s41598-023-28179-x](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-023-28179-x)

**Unrelated diversification**
- Pinheiro, Hartmann, Boschma and Hidalgo (2022), "The time and frequency of unrelated diversification", Research Policy 51(8):104323: unrelated diversification is rare and more frequent at intermediate levels of development. [M] [doi:10.1016/j.respol.2021.104323](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.respol.2021.104323)
- Nomaler and Verspagen (2023): specialisations lost often had higher product complexity than those gained, so smart specialisation should aim to preserve some existing specialisations as well as gain new ones; unrelated variety also matters for density-derived indicators such as the complexity outlook index. [V] [arXiv:2303.14458](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.14458)

**A generative model behind relatedness and nestedness**
- Hausmann and Hidalgo (2011), "The network structure of economic output", J. Econ. Growth 16(4):309–342: a tripartite model in which countries hold capabilities and products require them; a country makes a product exactly when it holds every capability the product requires. With N_a capabilities, each held by country c with probability r_c and required by a product with probability q, the probability that c makes a random product is (1 − q(1 − r_c))^{N_a}, so expected diversity is N_p (1 − q(1 − r_c))^{N_a}; the model reproduces the negative diversity–ubiquity relation and nestedness, and convex returns to accumulating capabilities ("quiescence trap"). [M] [doi:10.1007/s10887-011-9071-4](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10887-011-9071-4)
- Alshamsi, Pinheiro and Hidalgo (2018), "Optimal diversification strategies in the networks of related products and of related research areas", Nature Communications 9:1328: diversification modelled as relatedness-dependent activation on a network; the optimal targeting strategy depends on the strength of relatedness and on network structure. [M] [doi:10.1038/s41467-018-03740-9](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-018-03740-9)
- Jara-Figueroa, Jun, Glaeser and Hidalgo (2018), "The role of industry-specific, occupation-specific, and location-specific knowledge in the growth and survival of new firms", PNAS 115(50):12646–12653: Brazilian pioneer firms grow and survive more when their hires bring industry-specific knowledge. [M] [doi:10.1073/pnas.1800475115](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1800475115)

#### Inferences
- All co-occurrence relatedness measures are outcome-based: the same data that define φ also define M, so prediction tests must hold out time (fit φ on years ≤ t, predict entry in t+Δ) or space (fit on other regions), as Li and Neffke do. An implementation should make the hold-out a property of the estimator, like tyche's blocked cross-validation.
- Proximity, density, Neffke–Henning's ratio and association-strength normalisations are all functions of a bipartite incidence matrix; they name no object in nature and belong in a foundation (tyche `networks` holds bipartite network statistics already), with the domain holding regions, industries and their classifications.
- The capability model gives closed forms for expected diversity and ubiquity, making it a test generator for ECI/FC code (simulate M from known capabilities, check recovery of the ordering).

#### Gaps
- Typical effect sizes of density on entry (marginal probability per unit density) were not verified.
- The exact predictive margin of machine-learning entry prediction over density (AUC or precision figures) was not verified.
- The numbers in Pinheiro et al. (2022) on the share of unrelated entries were not verified.

### 3. Economic Complexity: the Method of Reflections, ECI, Fitness–Complexity, Critiques, Prediction and City Scale

#### Takeaway
ECI is the eigenvector of the second-largest eigenvalue of the row-stochastic matrix M̃ = D⁻¹ M U⁻¹ Mᵀ, and Mealy, Farmer and Teytelboym show it is exactly the relaxed normalised-cut solution of spectral clustering on the similarity graph S = M U⁻¹ Mᵀ, orthogonal to diversity by construction; Fitness–Complexity is a nonlinear fixed point (harmonic weighting by the least-fit producers) with convergence pathologies that a stable inhomogeneous map repairs. Both have been taken to regions and cities (UK local authorities, US states and metros, Chinese provinces), where complex activities concentrate super-linearly in large cities.

#### Cited Findings

**Method of reflections and ECI**
- Hidalgo and Hausmann (2009), "The building blocks of economic complexity", PNAS 106(26):10570–10575: k_{c,0} = Σ_p M_cp (diversity), k_{p,0} = Σ_c M_cp (ubiquity); k_{c,N} = (1/k_{c,0}) Σ_p M_cp k_{p,N−1}, k_{p,N} = (1/k_{p,0}) Σ_c M_cp k_{c,N−1}; the iteration converges to a trivial constant, the information lying in the rankings at intermediate N; complexity predicts growth beyond income. [M] [doi:10.1073/pnas.0900943106](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0900943106)
- Eigenvector formulation (as stated by Mealy et al.): D, U diagonal matrices of diversity and ubiquity; M̃ = D⁻¹ M U⁻¹ Mᵀ, entries M̃_cc' = (1/k_c) Σ_p M_cp M_c'p / k_p, row-stochastic (a Markov transition matrix); ECI is the eigenvector of M̃ for the second-largest right eigenvalue; PCI is the second eigenvector of M̂ = U⁻¹ Mᵀ D⁻¹ M, which shares M̃'s eigenvalues. [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)
- Proposition 1 (Mealy et al.): a country's ECI equals the average PCI of the products in which it has RCA > 1; ECI is obtained from PCI by ỹ = D⁻¹ M ŷ. [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)
- The Atlas standardises: ECI = (K − ⟨K⟩)/stdev(K); Mealy et al. use the unstandardised vector throughout. [M for the Atlas formula; V that Mealy use unstandardised] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)

**ECI as spectral clustering (Mealy, Farmer and Teytelboym 2019)**
- Published as "Interpreting economic complexity", Science Advances 5(1):eaau1705 (2019). [M for journal details] [doi:10.1126/sciadv.aau1705](https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aau1705); preprint read [V] [arXiv:1711.08245](https://arxiv.org/abs/1711.08245)
- Similarity matrix S = M U⁻¹ Mᵀ, so M̃ = D⁻¹ S; each row sum of S equals the country's diversity. cut(A, Ā) = Σ_{i∈A, j∈Ā} S_ij; Ncut(A, Ā) = (1/vol(A) + 1/vol(Ā)) cut(A, Ā), vol(A) = Σ_{i∈A} d_i. Relaxing the NP-hard Ncut gives (D − S)y = λDy; with the normalised Laplacian L̄_S = D^(−1/2)(D − S)D^(−1/2), the second-smallest eigenvector z^[2] (normalised Fiedler vector) gives y^[2] = D^(−1/2) z^[2]; since λ̃ = 1 − λ, ECI equals y^[2]. [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)
- By Shi and Malik, ECI minimises Σ_ij (y_i − y_j)² S_ij / Σ_i y_i² d_i subject to Σ_i y_i d_i = 0: orthogonality to diversity is hard-wired. ECI is related to diffusion maps and correspondence analysis. [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)
- ECI and diversity have zero dot product but need not be uncorrelated (neither has zero mean): positively correlated for country exports and Chinese regions, negatively for UK local authorities (industries), insignificant for US states (occupations). [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)
- Regional applications: UK BRES 2011, local authorities × 3-digit industries, binary matrix from LQ > 1, ECI positively correlated with per-capita earnings (ASHE), high ECI urban and financial/professional, low ECI rural, agricultural and manufacturing; US IPUMS 2010, states × 3-digit occupations, ECI correlated with state GDP per capita (BEA). In regional data diversity is not positively correlated with earnings or GDP per capita, and the triangular (nested) diversity–ubiquity structure is absent. [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)
- Caveats stated: the networks do not partition cleanly into two clusters (the eigengap heuristic indicates one cluster in all three datasets); results depend on the RCA threshold, correlation with GDP per capita highest for thresholds between about 0.5 and 2, and at threshold 0 ECI and diversity are perfectly correlated; conclusions rest on correlations. [V] [ar5iv 1711.08245](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1711.08245)

**Fitness–Complexity**
- Tacchella, Cristelli, Caldarelli, Gabrielli and Pietronero (2012), "A new metrics for countries' fitness and products' complexity", Scientific Reports 2:723; a non-monetary metric defined by the fixed point of coupled nonlinear maps. [S] [PMC3467565](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3467565), [doi:10.1038/srep00723](https://doi.org/10.1038/srep00723)
- The map (as restated by Servedio et al.): F_c^(n) = Σ_p M_cp Q_p^(n−1); Q_p^(n) = (Σ_c M_cp / F_c^(n−1))^(−1); F^(0) = Q^(0) = 1; since the map is invariant under a common rescaling, each iteration is normalised so that Σ_c F_c = 𝒞 and Σ_p Q_p = 𝒫. [V] [ar5iv 1807.10276](https://ar5iv.arxiv.org/html/1807.10276)
- Problems identified: if the "belly" of the ordered matrix is inward some fitnesses converge to zero (citing Pugliese et al. 2016); countries exporting nothing get zero fitness; in a fully specialised world every country and product gets unit values; the equations are asymmetric. [V] [ar5iv 1807.10276](https://ar5iv.arxiv.org/html/1807.10276)
- Pugliese, Zaccaria and Pietronero (2016), "On the convergence of the Fitness-Complexity algorithm", Eur. Phys. J. Special Topics 225:1893–1911. [M] [doi:10.1140/epjst/e2015-50118-1](https://doi.org/10.1140/epjst/e2015-50118-1)
- Servedio, Buttà, Mazzilli, Tacchella and Pietronero (2018), "A new and stable estimation method of country economic fitness and product complexity" (Entropy 20(10):783, [M] [doi:10.3390/e20100783](https://doi.org/10.3390/e20100783); open copy [S] [PMC7512345](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7512345)): with P_p = 1/Q_p the map becomes symmetric, F_c = Σ_p M_cp/P_p, P_p = Σ_c M_cp/F_c; adding positive terms φ_c, π_p and taking φ = π = δ → 0 after rescaling gives the parameter-free map F̃_c = Σ_p M_cp/P̃_p, P̃_p = 1 + Σ_c M_cp/F̃_c, needing no normalisation; fixed-point conditions F_c ≥ φ_c, P_p ≥ π_p, F_c P_p > M_cp; approximate analytic solution F̃ ≈ D − K D⁻¹ (row sums), P̃ ≈ 1 + Mᵀ D⁻¹, K = M Mᵀ the co-production matrix, error below 3%; local asymptotic stability by a Jacobian of spectral radius below one; rankings match the original. Uniqueness of the fixed point is not proved there. [V] [ar5iv 1807.10276](https://ar5iv.arxiv.org/html/1807.10276)
- Mariani, Vidmer, Medo and Zhang (2015), "Measuring economic complexity of countries and products: which metric to use?", Eur. Phys. J. B 88:293: Fitness–Complexity orders nested matrices better than the method of reflections. [M] [doi:10.1140/epjb/e2015-60298-7](https://doi.org/10.1140/epjb/e2015-60298-7)
- Morrison, Buldyrev, Imbruno, Doria Arrieta, Rungi, Riccaboni and Pammolli (2017), "On economic complexity and the fitness of nations", Scientific Reports 7:15332: Fitness–Complexity related to Sinkhorn–Knopp matrix balancing. [M] [doi:10.1038/s41598-017-14603-6](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-017-14603-6)
- Albeaik, Kaltenberg, Alsaleh and Hidalgo (2017), "Improving the economic complexity index" (ECI+), arXiv:1707.05826. [M] [arXiv:1707.05826](https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.05826)
- Pintar and Essletzbichler (2022): relatedness has a fairly common standard, but competing knowledge-complexity measures exist and implementation choices change regional complexity rankings and therefore policy conclusions. [S] [WU discussion paper geo-disc-2022_04](https://www-sre.wu.ac.at/sre-disc/geo-disc-2022_04.pdf)

**Predictive power for growth**
- Tacchella, Mazzilli and Pietronero (2018), "A dynamical systems approach to gross domestic product forecasting", Nature Physics 14:861–865: countries in the fitness–GDP-per-capita plane move along a laminar flow in some regions and chaotically in others; a "selective predictability scheme" (method of analogues) forecasts five-year growth better than IMF forecasts (I recall a margin of about 25%). [M] [doi:10.1038/s41567-018-0204-y](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41567-018-0204-y)
- Reviews: Hidalgo (2021), "Economic complexity theory and applications", Nature Reviews Physics 3:92–113 [M] [doi:10.1038/s42254-020-00275-1](https://doi.org/10.1038/s42254-020-00275-1); Balland, Broekel, Diodato, Giuliani, Hausmann, O'Clery and Rigby (2022), "The new paradigm of economic complexity", Research Policy 51(3):104450 [M] [doi:10.1016/j.respol.2021.104450](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.respol.2021.104450); Hidalgo (2023), "The policy implications of economic complexity", Research Policy 52(9):104863 [M] [doi:10.1016/j.respol.2023.104863](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.respol.2023.104863).

**City and regional scale**
- Balland and Rigby (2017), "The geography of complex knowledge", Economic Geography 93(1):1–23: knowledge complexity of US metros from patent classes by the method of reflections. [M] [doi:10.1080/00130095.2016.1205947](https://doi.org/10.1080/00130095.2016.1205947)
- Balland, Jara-Figueroa, Petralia, Steijn, Rigby and Hidalgo (2020), "Complex economic activities concentrate in large cities", Nature Human Behaviour 4:248–254: urban scaling exponents of activities (patents, industries, occupations, research areas) increase with their complexity. [M] [doi:10.1038/s41562-019-0803-3](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-019-0803-3)
- Gomez-Lievano, Patterson-Lomba and Hausmann (2017), "Explaining the prevalence, scaling and variance of urban phenomena", Nature Human Behaviour 1:0012: a phenomenon occurs in a city only if all its necessary factors are present; the number of required factors (complexity) sets both the scaling exponent with population and the lognormal variance across cities. [M] [doi:10.1038/s41562-016-0012](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-016-0012)
- Gao and Zhou (2018), "Quantifying China's regional economic complexity", Physica A 492:1591–1603. [M] [doi:10.1016/j.physa.2017.11.084](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.physa.2017.11.084)

#### Inferences
- An implementation should compute ECI through the symmetric matrix D^(−1/2) S D^(−1/2) (similar to M̃), on calx's symmetric eigensolver, and expose the spectral-clustering reading (Fiedler vector, eigengap) rather than the iteration; the method of reflections is then a power iteration on M̃ and a test of the eigen code.
- The infrastructure's tyche `networks` already holds "the bipartite ordering that the complexity index is" (tyche decision 0033, per CLAUDE.md); the sub-domain should reuse it and add only the economic objects (regions, classifications, RCA/LQ thresholds).
- Since regional data lack the nested structure of country exports (Mealy et al.), "complexity" of a city computed by ECI is a clustering coordinate, not a capability count; claims that a city is "more complex" should be reported with the eigengap and the threshold sensitivity.

#### Gaps
- The predictive-power debate (whether ECI/fitness predict growth beyond human capital, institutions and income) was not verified at any source in this session; the Nature Physics margin over the IMF is from memory.
- The published Science Advances version of Mealy et al. returned 403; journal pagination is from memory.

### 4. The Evolution of Technologies: Recombination, the Adjacent Possible, Patents, Heaps' and Zipf's Laws, and Urban Diversity

#### Takeaway
