- Identity: on random records (random weights, characters, parent maps, entrants, exiters), the terms sum to ΔZ exactly in rationals; the two-level sum equals the one-level ΔZ.
- Fisher in Price form: under pure proportional selection with exact copying and z = fitness, transmission = 0 and ΔZ = Var(w)/W (size-weighted), exactly.
- Replicator: one generation of roulette-wheel selection in expectation equals p_i f_i / f̄.
- Neutrality: with fitness-blind selection the selection term has expectation zero; the size change in a GP run then equals the operators' transmission bias (Langdon and Poli's bloat argument).
- Breeder's equation: truncation selection of the top fraction q of a normal character with variance σ² has selection differential S = σ φ(Φ⁻¹(1−q))/q, and response h²S with heritability h² (Mühlenbein) [M for the GA literature; the formula is standard quantitative genetics].
- Firms: a hand-worked panel of five firms (two continuing, one entrant, one exiter, one with zero growth) reproducing Foster–Haltiwanger–Krizan's within, between, cross, entry and exit terms and Holm's equivalence.
- Information geometry: the IGO step on a Bernoulli family equals PBIL's update in closed form; on an isotropic Gaussian it gives the known natural-gradient mean update.
- Weak selection: the replicator step and multiplicative weights agree to first order in the selection strength (Chastain et al.).
- Models with known answers for GA-learning agents: Muth's cobweb REE price (linear demand and supply, closed form), reached exactly with the election operator and approached only in a neighbourhood without it; Vriend's Cournot game with linear demand, Cournot quantity (individual learning) and Walrasian quantity (social learning), both closed forms, the individual case flagged as contested; Kiyotaki–Wright's fundamental equilibrium selected (MMS 1990), against the QuantEcon replication.

**What is worth building first.**
1. The Price decomposition over genealogies, with entry by source and transmission by operator, multilevel and multi-interval, and the checks above. Cheap, exact, general, and it gives at once an accounting of what outside proposals contributed to a search and a productivity decomposition for any panel of firms.
2. GA-learning agents in the cobweb and Cournot models, with social versus individual learning as a declared strategy of the existing loop, and the election operator as one survival strategy; their convergence (and non-convergence) are known answers.
3. A bilevel policy search: the existing loop as the planner over policies, inner agents re-adapting under each candidate, judged across an ensemble of agent models and unidentified parameters, with the existing surrogate screening for the costly inner economy.
4. Language-model agents only as proposers of behaviours and policies (grade C), validated against the same known-answer games before any use as a population.
5. Deferred: a Santa Fe-type market (heavy, operator-sensitive; build only with the corrected mutation operator and operator-invariance reported), open-endedness measures (MODES-style) on quality-diversity runs read as innovation, and causal attribution of innovation by counterfactual runs or Shapley values over sources.

#### Gaps
- No published implementation computes Price decompositions over arbitrary EC genealogies with external proposers; the design above is an inference from Holm (2014), Bassett et al. (2004) and Langdon and Poli (1997), not a reported system.
- Causal (counterfactual) attribution of innovation in evolutionary runs has no standard method in the literature found.

## 6. Synthesis

### 6.1 What the Five Parts Establish Together

The five Parts agree on the shape of the answer. **Evolutionary economics offers one exact formal core, a handful of analytic models with closed forms, many simulation models whose validation is weak, a body of measurements whose evidence is correlational, and a methodological record of how evolutionary search goes wrong in economics.** Each of these has a different standing, and an infrastructure must keep them apart.

**The core is a population with a genealogy.** Every Part works with the same object: units carrying weights (capacity, output, employment, market or budget shares, copies in a population), traits (unit cost, productivity, routines, strategies, rules, a portfolio of activities), a correspondence from one census to the next, and three kinds of operator.

- **Variation:** search by R&D drawing innovations or imitating best practice (Part I §1; Part II §1), innovation as a Poisson process and imitation as logistic contact (Part I §5), mutation and crossover of rules (Part V §1), recombination of components and the expansion of the adjacent possible (Part IV §4), the revision of a strategy by a protocol (Part III §1), the proposal of a policy variant (Part III §5).
- **Selection:** differential growth by a replicator in fitness, competitiveness or unit cost (Part I §2; Part II §1), profit-financed investment (Part I §1), exit at zero share or negative net worth (Part II §1), entry and exit of activities in regions conditioned by relatedness (Part IV §2), budgets reallocated among programmes (Part III §6).
- **Retention:** routines and habits as replicators (Part I §4), conventions and norms as stochastically stable equilibria (Part III §2, §4), institutions as summary representations of an equilibrium (Part III §4), spinoffs inheriting their parents' capabilities (Part IV §1; Part V §5).

Price's equation accounts exactly for any record of this form (Part I §2 to §4; Part V §3, §5), which makes it the shared core of evolution as object and evolution as method (§6.2).

**The analytic models are few, and they are the checks.**

- Metcalfe's replicator dynamics of capacity shares and Fisher's principle (Part I §2);
- Iwai's logistic imitation with Poisson innovation, converging to "a statistical equilibrium of technological disequilibria" (Part I §5);
- Klepper's life-cycle propositions (Part I §5);
- the revision-protocol dynamics of evolutionary game theory, with their stability theorems in potential and contractive games and closed-form cycling in Rock–Paper–Scissors and Mismatching Pennies (Part III §1);
- stochastic stability, with exact stationary distributions where the process is reversible (two strategies; potential games under logit choice) and the selection of risk-dominant conventions (Part III §2);
- cultural transmission, with Bisin and Verdier's globally stable heterogeneous state (Part III §4);
- the urn and growth processes of Simon, Yule, Pólya and Arthur, Tria et al., Youn et al., Gibrat and Kesten, and the capability model of Hausmann and Hidalgo (Part IV §2, §5).

**The simulation models are many, and their validation is the field's weak point.**

- The Keynes + Schumpeter family claims validity by reproducing 17 listed stylised facts over 100 Monte Carlo runs, judged mostly by signs, orderings of volatilities and visual departures from Gaussianity; no numerical acceptance thresholds are stated, averaging across runs washes out path dependence, no full estimation on data was found, no ODD description and no seeded reference outputs exist, and no independent replication of a K+S policy result was found (Part II §1, §3 to §6).
- Estimation methods work on models of tens of parameters: a likelihood-based Bayesian estimator beats simulated minimum distance in Platt's benchmark but degrades as models grow; surrogates locate regions where declared criteria hold; statistical model checking corrected "some erroneous conclusions" of earlier analyses (Part II §4).
- Nelson and Winter's programs were never fully documented and survive in reconstructions (Part I §1; Part II §2).
- History-friendly models match one industry's dated history with many free parameters (Part II §2).
- Generative social simulations with language-model agents are validated mostly by believability (Part V §4).

**The measurements predict but do not identify.** Relatedness predicts the entry of activities into places out of sample, every relatedness matrix helping, with a trade-off between predictive skill and interpretability (Part IV §2); the ECI is the relaxed normalised cut of a similarity graph, orthogonal to diversity by construction, and regional networks do not split cleanly into two clusters (Part IV §3); the evidence on smart specialisation is observational, with no untreated control group in the European Union (Part IV §6); missions and experimentalist programmes are chosen non-randomly, and the OECD finds conventional evaluation ill-suited to them (Part III §5).

**Policy practice has a vocabulary without a law of motion.** Variety, selection and retention (Metcalfe), directionality (missions), discovery (Hausmann and Rodrik), recursive revision (Sabel and Zeitlin) are concepts; the closest formal objects are Callander's search on a Brownian landscape, Volden, Ting and Carpenter's learning and diffusion, and the dual control of active adaptive management (Part III §5). No agent-based model in which the policymaker's rule itself evolves by selection was verified, and no source couples a revision protocol over policy portfolios with a fitness that includes the value of information (Part III §5, §6).

**Evolution as method has a record of artefacts.** Genetic-algorithm learning reaches the rational-expectations equilibrium where it is stable under learning and the operators favour convergence (Arifovic's election operator); it does not where the equilibrium is indeterminate, where learning is social and payoffs relative (Vriend's spite effect, contested in its individual-learning half), or where learning is fast; the Santa Fe market's celebrated regime of technical trading was inflated by a biased mutation operator; trading rules found by search lose their edge out of sample once costs are counted (Part V §1, §2, §6). Bilevel policy search with adaptive agents (the AI Economist, the LLM Economist) answers the Lucas critique inside the model and not outside it (Part V §4).

**Where the Parts converge.**

- Parts I and V reach the same identity from two directions: Part I derives FHK as Price's equation [D], and Part V reads Holm's derivation in full [V] (§0.4, item 5).
- Parts I, III and V meet in the replicator: the replicator of shares (Part I §2), the replicator as the mean dynamic of imitation (Part III §1), and proportional selection as a discrete replicator (Part V §3).
- Parts I and IV both treat Iwai, Winter and Klepper; Part IV adds Klepper's spinoff genealogies, which Part V treats as lineages.
- Parts II and V, with the earlier survey's Parts V and VII, meet in Lamperti, Roventini and Sani's surrogates, whose role in the allocation domain is already built (ADR 0140, ADR 0142).
- Parts IV and V meet in Tria et al.'s urn, read once as a model of urban and technological novelty and once as a model of open-endedness.
- Parts III and V meet in learning: fictitious play, reinforcement and genetic-algorithm learning are each the discrete counterpart of a population dynamic (Part III §3; Part V §1).

### 6.2 The Price Equation as the Shared Core of Evolution as Object and as Method

**The identity.** For a set P whose elements i have amounts ω_i and properties x_i, transformed into a corresponding set P′, Price's equation reads ē·ΔX = Cov(e_i, x_i) + E(e_i Δx_i), with ω_i′ = ω_i e_i/ē, e_i the fitness of element i and ē the average fitness (Hodgson and Knudsen's appendix, after Price 1995 and Frank 1997; Part I §2 [V]). In the form used for firms, with size x_i, character z_i, share s_i = x_i/X, fitness w_i = x′_i/x_i and mean fitness W = X′/X, it reads W ΔZ = Cov(w_i, z_i) + E(w_i Δz_i), the first term "selection / inter-firm / reallocation", the second "innovation / learning / intra-firm" (Holm 2014, eq. 3; Part V §3 [V]). It holds for any population with a defined ancestor–descendant correspondence; it is a tautology whose use lies in the decomposition and the invariances it exposes, not in prediction (Frank 2012; Part V §3, §6).

**With entry and exit.** Distinguishing continuing (C), entering (N) and exiting (X) units, Holm's eq. 4 reads ΔZ = Σ_{i∈C} Δs_i (z_i − Z) [selection] + Σ_{i∈C} s′_i Δz_i [learning] + Σ_{i∈N} s′_i (z′_i − Z) [entry] − Σ_{i∈X} s_i (z_i − Z) [exit] (Part V §5 [V]). It is an identity, since shares sum to one in both censuses (checked in exact rationals on 200 random records [D, assembler]).

**Its instances.** The five Parts hold six readings of the one identity.

1. **Fisher's principle.** Setting the trait equal to fitness, constant over the step, gives Δē = Var(e)/ē, and in continuous time with time-invariant fitness dφ/dt = Var_s(f) (Part I §2 [D]). Metcalfe's economic form tracks a cost trait under a linear fitness g_i = δ(p − h_i): dh_s/dt = −δ·Var_s(h) + E_s(dh/dt), exact for any common price p, so that it survives the frequency dependence that enters through the price (Part I §2 [D]). Fisher's statement holds exactly only when the transmission term vanishes; otherwise only the "partial change" reading of Fisher's theorem holds (Part I §2; Part V §3).
2. **The replicator.** The discrete step s_i′ = s_i f_i/f̄ is pure selection with exact copying (Part I §7; Part V §3, §7); the continuous replicator ds_i/dt = s_i(f_i − φ), φ = Σ_j s_j f_j, is its limit (Part I §2 [V]); in Sandholm's framework it is the mean dynamic of pairwise proportional imitation, ẋ_i = x_i F̂_i(x) (Part III §1 [V]). For the replicator, Sandholm's positive correlation V′F equals Σ_i x_i(F_i − F̄)F_i = Var_x(F): the incentive property on which the convergence theory of Part III rests is, for this dynamic, Fisher's variance [D, assembler]. For other dynamics, positive correlation is a covariance under the uniform distribution on strategies, Cov = (1/n)V′F (Part III §1 [V]), not Price's share-weighted covariance; the two must not be confused.
3. **The productivity decompositions.** For survivors without entry and exit, FHK's "between" term is Price's selection covariance, and its "within" and "cross" terms split Price's transmission term; BHC absorbs the cross term into "between" (Part I §3 [D]; Holm, Part V §5 [V]). Exit fits Price, since exiters have w_i = 0. Entry does not: entrants have no ancestor in Price's corresponding set, so every decomposition adds an entry term whose value depends on the reference chosen; since shares sum to one, any reference Φ_REF yields an exact identity (Melitz and Polanec, Part I §3 [V]). The reference carries meaning. Melitz and Polanec's dynamic Olley–Pakes decomposition judges entrants against survivors at t′ and exiters against survivors at t, so that the survivors' contribution is the change that would have been observed without entry and exit; FHK, and Holm with it, judge both against the pre-evolution mean and over-measure entry whenever productivity grows (in the representative-firm case, entry s_E γ under FHK, s_E γ/2 under Griliches–Regev, 0 under DOPD; Part I §3 [V]); Baldwin and Gu argue that entrants should be compared with exiters (Part V §5 [V]). Olley and Pakes's covariance is a level statistic of one cross-section, not Price's covariance between fitness and trait (Part I §3).
4. **Multilevel selection.** Price's expectation term can be expanded by Price's equation again (Hodgson and Knudsen, Part I §2 [V]): with groups k, w̄Δz̄ = Cov_k(W_k, Z_k) + E_k[Cov_{i|k}(w_ik, z_ik)] + E_k[E_{i|k}(w_ik Δz_ik)], between-group selection, within-group selection and within-member transmission (Part I §4 [D]). Holm derives the two-level version for firms within industries (Part V §3 [V]). Sector, firm and plant are its economic levels; islands, layers, niches and quality-diversity cells are its levels in a search (Part V §3, §7).
5. **Selection inside evolutionary computation.** Altenberg shows that the schema theorem says nothing about how well a genetic algorithm performs, its interpretations resting on a correlation between parental and offspring fitness that Price's covariance makes explicit; Langdon and Poli show with Price's theorem that fitness-based selection causes the growth of program size; Poli and McPhee rewrite their size-evolution equation in Price's form to set a parsimony coefficient; Bassett, Potter and De Jong use an extension of Price's equation to look inside production-level algorithms whose black-box behaviour is alike (Part V §3 [V record]).
6. **Selection as information geometry.** Information-geometric optimisation turns any smooth family of search distributions into a natural-gradient optimiser, recovering a version of CMA-ES and xNES for Gaussians and PBIL and the compact genetic algorithm for Bernoulli families (Part V §3 [V record]); the replicator is a natural-gradient flow of mean fitness in the Shahshahani metric and formally Bayes's rule with fitness as likelihood (Part V §3 [M]); under weak selection, population genetics with recombination coincides with multiplicative-weights learning (Chastain et al., Part V §3 [V abstract], its reading of diversity disputed by Meir and Parkes).

**One record, many meanings.** The identity needs a census at two times, a weight, a character and a parent map; what changes between instances is what these mean. Table 6.1 sets the instances side by side; the last two rows are design readings of this survey, not findings of the literature.

**Table 6.1.** One census record read in seven settings.

| Setting | Units | Weight | Character | Parent map | Entrants | Groups | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Industry (Part I §3; Part V §5) | firms or plants | employment, output, value added | productivity, unit cost | identity of continuing firms; spinoffs to parents (Klepper) | new firms | sector, region | Part I §3; Part IV §1 |
| Routines (Part I §1, §4) | routines carried by firms | capital or capacity of carriers | behavioural parameters | imitation (horizontal) | innovations, entrants | firms as interactors | Part I §4 |
| Games (Part III §1) | strategies | population shares | payoffs | revision by a protocol | unused strategies taken up | populations | Part III §1 |
| Learning agents (Part V §1) | rules held by agents | copies in a rule population | realised payoff | genetic operators | mutants, outside rules | one population (social) or one per agent (individual) | Part V §1 |
| Search (Part V §3, §7) | candidate solutions | copies, archive share | fitness or any descriptor | parents' tickets with transmission weights | outside proposals, immigrants | islands, layers, niches | Part V §5, §7 |
| Policy portfolios (Part III §6) | instruments or programme variants | budget shares | expected net value under the posterior | variants of a programme | new pilots | jurisdictions, missions | design reading |
| Projects (the generative ecology) | lineages of agents | the conserved resource a lineage holds | any measured property of its genome or body | division and reseeding | founders, channels from outside | gardens | design reading |

**What the identity does not give.**

- **Causation.** Selection is always a causal claim; the covariance identifies selection only when it differs significantly from zero, and the causes of differential elimination must be supplied separately (Hodgson and Knudsen, Part I §6 [V]). A decomposition cannot say whether shares moved because of productivity or because of demand or prices (Part I §6); revenue productivity confounds selection on efficiency with selection on prices, since entrants charge lower prices (Melitz and Polanec, citing Foster et al. 2008; Part I §3, §6 [V]).
- **Additivity over intervals.** The selection terms of successive short intervals do not sum to the selection term of the whole interval, so that results depend on the census years; Bottazzi et al.'s strong selection is partly an artefact of summing up to fifteen one-year decompositions (Holm, Part V §5 [V]).
- **Independence of the scale.** Decompositions in logs and in levels differ, and a 2023 paper recommends levels (Part V §5 [S], venue unverified, §0.4 item 2).
- **Independence of the comparison group.** Under social learning a rule's fitness is compared across firms, so that a rule that lowers everyone's profit but its rivals' more is selected; under individual learning it is compared within a firm's own population; the two converge to different outputs (Vriend, Part V §1 [S], the individual case contested). In Price's terms the group over which mean fitness is taken is part of the model, and the record must declare it.
- **Replenishment.** Subset selection (one cycle of differential elimination) is a contraction mapping that runs dry of variation; generative selection (replication with variation) is not (Hodgson and Knudsen, Part I §4 [V]). Firm shakeouts and bankruptcies are subset selection; a separate source of variation (entry, imitation) is needed (Part I §4, §6). A search that only retires without varying behaves the same way.
- **Causal credit for entrants.** The entry term split by source gives the exact accounting contribution of outside proposals to the change of a population's mean, and their descendants' later contributions can be carried along lineages through the transmission term; it does not say what would have happened without them (Part V §5). Causal credit needs counterfactual runs or a Shapley value over sources, and neither was found in the literature on evolutionary search (Part V §5).
- **The second moment.** Selection can change the variance without changing the mean (Andersen and Holm, Part I §2 [S]); a Price module should compute the analogous identity for (z − z̄)², with the correction for the shift of the mean, not the mean alone (Part I §2).

### 6.3 The Standing of Each Result: Identity, Theorem, Model Proposition or Simulation Finding

Part I §6 proposes four kinds of result; the Parts together need six. Table 6.2 classifies the main results of all five Parts. A failing check of an identity is a bug; a theorem's hypotheses are checked before its conclusion is asserted; a model proposition is checked by simulation against its analytic value; a simulation finding is reported as a distribution over runs with its spread, seeds and run-count control; an empirical regularity or finding is a target with its data, never a law.

**Table 6.2.** The standing of the main results.

| Kind | Results | Where | Strongest marker |
|---|---|---|---|
| Identity | Price's equation, single-level and multilevel; Holm's form with entry and exit | Part I §2, §4; Part V §3, §5 | [V] |
| Identity | every productivity decomposition (BHC, GR, FHK, OP, DOPD) sums to ΔΦ for any reference | Part I §3 | [V] |
| Identity | H = UV + RV (the chain rule of entropy) | Part IV §1 | [M] for the measure; the chain rule is exact |
| Identity | M̃ row-stochastic; ECI has zero dot product with diversity; ECI = average PCI of the products made (Proposition 1); Tria's urn size N₀ + (ν + 1)D + ρt | Part IV §3, §4 | [V] |
| Identity (construction) | every well-behaved revision protocol defines a mean dynamic (Sandholm eq. 13.3) | Part III §1 | [V] |
| Theorem under hypotheses | Fisher's principle (no transmission, fitness fixed); Metcalfe's form for any common price | Part I §2 | [D] |
| Theorem under hypotheses | Kurtz's finite-horizon approximation (not extendable to an infinite horizon); Benaïm and Weibull's exponential rate | Part III §1 | [V] |
| Theorem under hypotheses | potential games: the potential is a strict Lyapunov function under positive correlation, and asymptotic stability equals isolated local maximisation under PC and NS (Theorems 13.6, 13.7); contractive games: NE = GNSS, convex, with one Lyapunov function per canonical dynamic (Theorem 13.9, Table 13.4); dominated strategies survive under any continuous dynamic with PC, NS and innovation in some games, and imitative dynamics eliminate them on interior solutions (knife-edge) | Part III §1 | [V] |
| Theorem under hypotheses | the birth–death stationary distribution (Theorem 5.3); agreement of the small-noise and large-population limits (Theorem 7.7); risk dominance and stochastic stability with linear payoffs (Corollaries 7.11, 7.12); the Gibbs distribution of logit play in potential games (Young, Theorem 1; Blume) | Part III §2 | [V] |
| Theorem under hypotheses | Ellison's radius and modified coradius; Young's adaptive play and resistance trees; Hart and Mas-Colell's instability under uncoupled protocols | Part III §1, §2 | [V abstract] for Ellison; [V ref] for Hart and Mas-Colell; [M] for the statements of Ellison's and Young's theorems |
| Theorem under hypotheses | NK landscapes: unique optimum at K = 0 reached within N improving steps; local optimum with probability 1/(N + 1) on an uncorrelated landscape; global optimisation NP-complete, local optimisation PLS-complete for K > 1 | Part I §5 | [D]; [S] for the complexity results |
| Theorem under hypotheses | Simon and Yule's exponents; Kesten's tail E[a^ζ] = 1; Pólya's Beta limit; Arthur's lock-in; Barabási and Albert's exponent 3; Tria's Heaps and Zipf exponents | Part IV §4, §5 | [V] for Tria; [M] for the rest |
| Theorem under hypotheses | information-geometric optimisation recovers CMA-ES, xNES, PBIL and the compact genetic algorithm; weak selection equals multiplicative weights; Cross's reinforcement tends to the replicator; fictitious play is a discrete best-response dynamic | Part III §3; Part V §3 | [V record] or [V abstract]; [V] for the learning correspondences via Sandholm |
| Model proposition | Iwai's statistical equilibrium of technological disequilibria; Klepper's shakeout and cohort propositions; Bisin and Verdier's globally stable heterogeneous state; Cavalli-Sforza and Feldman's logistic dynamics; Melitz and Polanec's representative-firm case; the capability model's expected diversity; Gomez-Lievano et al.'s scaling from necessary factors; Callander's policymaking that can get stuck; Volden, Ting and Carpenter's observational equivalence of diffusion and independent learning | Part I §3, §5; Part III §4, §5; Part IV §2, §3 | [V] for Melitz–Polanec, Bisin–Verdier and Iwai's abstract; [S] or [M] for the rest |
| Simulation finding | Nelson and Winter's growth model against Solow's data; Winter's regimes; Silverberg, Dosi and Orsenigo's diffusion; Levinthal's persistence of heterogeneity at high K; Arthur and Polak's stepping stones | Part I §1, §5; Part IV §4 | [M] |
| Simulation finding | the K+S family's 17 stylised facts and its policy tables (lower opportunities 0.774 of baseline growth; broader patents 0.647; strong antitrust volatility 0.628); the Fordist regime's higher and smoother growth; mission policies; the industry model's robust fat tails | Part II §1, §2, §6 | [V] for the OFCE tables; [S] for the rest |
| Simulation finding | GA convergence in the cobweb with the election operator; Vriend's spite effect (contested); the Santa Fe market's regimes (an artefact, corrected); Marimon, McGrattan and Sargent's fundamental equilibrium; Miller's coevolved punishing strategies; Bowles, Choi and Hopfensitz's coevolution of behaviour and institutions | Part III §4; Part V §1, §2 | [V record] or [S] |

### 6.4 Questions the Literature Leaves Open

- **The unit of selection.** Firms do not reproduce; routines spread by imitation and spinoffs; what counts as inheritance (and whether "Lamarckian" is the right word) is contested; very little is well defined about social replicators, interactors and the levels of social selection (Part I §4, §6). Micro–meso–macro has no equational form (Part I §4).
- **Reductions of the classical models.** No closed-form restatement of Nelson and Winter's chapter 12 model, and no mean-field or Fokker–Planck reduction of it, was found (Part I §1); no 2020–2026 review restates the Schumpeterian models in a common formalism (Part I §5).
- **Validation and estimation of evolutionary macro models.** No full estimation of K+S parameters on data, no ODD description, no seeded reference outputs, no formal acceptance bands for entry and exit or for life cycles, and no independent replication of a K+S policy result (Part II §1, §3 to §6); whether averaging across runs is legitimate where paths depend on history is an open "puzzle" the K+S authors concede (Part II §1).
- **Dynamics.** Whether imitative dynamics other than the replicator converge globally in contractive games is stated by Sandholm as an open question (Part III §1); closed-form waiting times in finite populations were not verified (Part III §2).
- **Norms and institutions.** How often observed norms coincide with the stochastically stable state, beyond Young's cases, is not known; identification from cross-sections is "extremely difficult" (Part III §4); no formal model of institutional change in Ostrom's or Aoki's sense was read (Part III §4).
- **Policy as an evolving object.** No agent-based model in which the policymaker's rule evolves by selection was verified; no source couples a revision protocol over portfolios with value-of-information fitness; no outcome figures for Horizon Europe's missions were read; policy labs' outcomes are unknown (Part III §5, §6).
- **Geography and complexity.** The equations of Frenken and Boschma's branching model, whether they derive Zipf analytically and whether the model was ever calibrated, are unverified (Part IV §1, §5); whether ECI or fitness predict growth beyond human capital, institutions and income was not verified (Part IV §3); no causal evaluation of smart specialisation was found (Part IV §6); no open dataset of establishments by fine industry matches NETS for cities worldwide (Part IV §7).
- **Evolution as method.** No published system computes Price decompositions over arbitrary genealogies of evolutionary computation with external proposers; no standard method attributes innovation causally in evolutionary runs; no multilevel Price decomposition of a MAP-Elites archive was found; no study documents the reproducibility of language-model agent economies across model versions; no peer-reviewed study of symbolic regression discovering a new economic law after 2015 was found (Part V §2 to §5, §7).
- **Recency.** Few verified items date from 2024 to 2026: the 2025 Cambridge Elements on the K+S family and on evolutionary selection, the 2024 *Annual Review* on industrial policy, the 2024 and 2025 OECD reports, Li and Neffke (2024), Larooij and Törnberg (2025), the LLM Economist (2025) and a few 2026 preprints (Part II §1; Part III §5; Part IV §2; Part V §4).

## 7. What It Means for the Design of a Computational Infrastructure

The following points gather the Parts' closing sections (Part I §7, Part II §7, Part III §6, Part IV §7, Part V §7) into one design, and say where each piece belongs under the infrastructure's rules: general methods in the foundations, objects that name something in nature in a domain, and whatever touches the world in the API, the edges and the works. The placements, names and order are inferences of this survey, offered for decision; none is a finding of the literature.

### 7.1 Placement Under the Order of the Infrastructure

**What the order allows.** The economics domain declares calx, morphe, physis, tyche and nomos, and no domain (`domains/economics/module.json`). The allocation domain stands on economics at height 1 and declares heuresis, kategoria, kinesis, archeion and apodeixis. The policy domain stands at height 0 on peira, tekmerion, nomos, tyche and apodeixis. kybernesis is a foundation and depends on no domain. Four consequences follow.

1. **The general methods go to the foundations both evolutions stand on.** The census record and the Price decomposition name no object in nature and serve evolution as object (firms) and evolution as method (candidate solutions); both heuresis and the economics domain stand on tyche, so tyche is their home, and heuresis's lineage is read into tyche's record rather than the reverse. Revision protocols, their dynamics and stochastic stability belong with nomos's `games` and `agents`, on which economics already stands. Urn and growth processes belong in tyche's `processes`; incidence-matrix measures in tyche's `networks`, beside the complexity ordering of its decision 0033, and in nomos's `measurement`; stylised-fact statistics and statistical model checking in tyche; NK landscapes in heuresis's `benchmarks`; a minimum-weight arborescence in morphe's combinatorics. Each is a change in that library's own repository, under its own CLAUDE.md.
2. **The sub-domain holds what names an economy.** Firms, plants, industries, routines as declared behavioural parameters, markets, regions, products, technologies, their classifications and crosswalks, the composed models (industry dynamics, the evolving economy of two sectors, economies of learning agents, diversification under relatedness) and the readers' schemas of firm panels. As a working proposal, three modules of the economics domain come first: `selection` (census records of firms and plants, and the productivity decompositions), `industry` (population and agent models of industries, later the K+S core), and `diversification` (relatedness, complexity, branching); a fourth, `adaptive`, holds economies of learning agents. The name `evolution` is avoided, since the allocation domain's module bears it. All names are provisional.
3. **One dependency is for the user to decide.** The models that vary routines, rules or technologies by evolutionary operators (genetic-algorithm learning, the K+S search as declared operators, NK search) need heuresis, which the economics domain does not declare. Declaring it closes no cycle, since heuresis rests on calx, morphe, physis and tyche only; the alternative, placing those models in a domain that already declares heuresis, would put economic objects where they do not belong. The change to `domains/economics/module.json` is proposed for the user's agreement, to be made with the first module that needs it, as kybernesis's dependency on archeion was (ADR 0131).
4. **The connections go through the API and the works.** The economics domain cannot use the allocation domain, which stands on it; joining economics and policy in a domain would make a bridge at height 1, which the rule of two occurrences (CLAUDE.md §2.2) argues against until the pattern recurs; kybernesis composes domains only through the API, as `science/steering.hpp` does. The connections of §7.6 are therefore compositions in `api/cpp/science/`, as `science/evolve.hpp` and `science/policy.hpp` already are.

### 7.2 Formal Objects

1. **A census record** (tyche). Two populations, at t and t′; for each member a weight x_i ≥ 0 and a character z_i (a scalar or a vector), in exact rationals where the data allow; a parent map from each member at t′ to its parents at t, with transmission weights (1 for clonal descent, ½ each for two-parent crossover, or as declared); members of t′ without parents are entrants (N), members of t without descendants are exiters (X), the rest continuing (C); optional group labels at declared levels; an operator label on each birth, a source label on each entrant, a reason on each exit; a declared reference for entry and exit (the incumbent mean, as FHK and Holm; survivors in the same census, as Melitz and Polanec's DOPD; exiters, as Baldwin and Gu) (Part I §7; Part V §5, §7).
2. **The Price decomposition** (tyche). Selection, transmission (split into within and cross, and by operator), entry (by source, against the declared reference) and exit (by reason); the multilevel recursion with every level's sum equal to the single-level change; the second-moment identity; each census pair and the whole span reported side by side, since they do not add; logs and levels both available; the terms reported as descriptive, never as causes (Part I §2 to §4, §6; Part V §3, §5, §7).
3. **The productivity decompositions** (economics, `selection`). BHC, Griliches–Regev, FHK, Olley–Pakes and DOPD, in logs and in levels, each returning components that sum to ΔΦ, each composed on the Price decomposition where it is one, with the reference written into the result (Part I §3, §7).
4. **The replicator family** (nomos). Continuous ds_i/dt = s_i(f_i − f̄); discrete s_i′ = s_i f_i/f̄; Maynard Smith's form; Metcalfe's linear fitness g_i = δ(p − h_i) with a common price; the capacity-bounded variant dx_i/dt = x_i(f_i − φ)(1 − Σ_{i∈k} x_i/z_k); K+S's quasi-replicator f_j,t = f_j,t−1(1 + χ(E_j,t − Ē_t)/Ē_t) with an exit threshold (Part I §2, §7; Part II §1).
5. **Revision protocols** (nomos). A protocol ρ(π, x) returning a nonnegative n × n matrix, with several populations; its mean dynamic ẋ_i = Σ_j x_j ρ_ji − x_i Σ_j ρ_ij; a finite-N Markov jump process built from the same protocol on nomos's Gillespie simulation; a differential-inclusion integrator for best response; the canonical protocols (replicator, best response, logit, BNN, Smith, projection, Maynard Smith, imitative logit, BRM(ε), probit) and the imitative, excess-payoff and pairwise-comparison families; positive correlation and Nash stationarity as laws answered with verdicts (holds, fails at x, undecided); potential and contractive tests with the Lyapunov function of Table 13.4 (Part III §1, §6).
6. **Stochastic stability** (nomos, tyche, morphe). Exact stationary distributions by linear algebra for small state spaces; the birth–death product formula; the Gibbs distribution of logit play in potential games and on networks; the stochastic potential by resistance trees, which needs a minimum-weight arborescence; Ellison's radius and modified coradius; waiting times by simulation (Part III §2, §6).
7. **Learning rules** (nomos's `agents`, as behaviours of the learned origin). Experience-weighted attraction with its special cases; Roth and Erev's reinforcement with forgetting and experimentation; stochastic fictitious play; genetic-algorithm learning on heuresis's loop with the population structure declared (one population shared by the agents, or one per agent) and Arifovic's election operator as a survival strategy; every fit preceded by a parameter-recovery report on simulated data (Part III §3, §6; Part V §1, §7).
8. **Cultural transmission and norms** (nomos, with tyche's `networks`). Cavalli-Sforza and Feldman's vertical and oblique transmission; Boyd and Richerson's frequency dependence; Bisin and Verdier's endogenous socialisation; norms on networks under log-linear response, with punctuated equilibrium, compression and local conformity with global diversity as outputs to be measured (Part III §4, §6).
9. **Industry models as Markov processes on firms' states** (economics, `industry`). Iwai's logistic imitation with Poisson innovation; Metcalfe's capacity dynamics; Nelson and Winter's chapter 12 model (Bernoulli search with probability proportional to R&D times capital, imitation of best practice, science-based or cumulative draws, a demand curve, an investment rule, entry); Klepper's cohorts with process R&D, cost spreading, adjustment costs and zero-profit entry; Silverberg, Dosi and Orsenigo's vintages with learning by using; organisational search on NK landscapes; each with declared parameters and seeds, selection a declared operator (replicator, profit-financed growth, exit threshold, imitation by distance), and the genealogy recorded as a census record (Part I §1, §5, §7; Part II §2). Metcalfe's replicator of shares rests on a source read in full (Heinrich, Yang and Dai) and Iwai's logistic law is a closed form; the exact forms of Nelson and Winter's, Klepper's and Silverberg, Dosi and Orsenigo's models are from memory and must be read at the source, or in the NELWIN and LSD codes, before they are written (Part I §7, Gaps).
10. **An evolving economy of two sectors** (economics, `industry`). The K+S core: capital-good firms with technology (A_i, B_i) searching by a Bernoulli success 1 − exp(−ζ·IN) and a Beta step on [x̲₁, x̄₁], imitation by technological distance, brochures to sampled clients; consumption-good firms with vintages, adaptive expectations, inventories, expansion and payback replacement, supplier choice on price plus unit cost, a debt-to-sales cap; the quasi-replicator on E = −ω₁p − ω₂l; a mark-up driven by share; exit at zero share or negative net worth with replacement by smaller entrants; an institutional wage rule; the six-step timing; every payment a posting on the economics domain's `ledger`, so that GDP = C + I + ΔN and the stock–flow identities hold by construction; banks, the Taylor rule, bail-outs and bonds as the second stage (Part II §1, §7).
11. **Urn and growth processes** (tyche's `processes`). One parameterised urn with innovation giving Simon, Yule, Pólya and Arthur, Tria's triggering with its semantic variant, and Youn's innovation rate α(N) ≈ x₀/N as special cases; Gibrat and Kesten; preferential attachment; Weitzman's recombinant growth and the equation of the adjacent possible; exact recursions for E[D(t)] beside the asymptotic exponents (Part IV §4, §5, §7).
12. **Incidence-matrix measures** (tyche's `networks`, nomos's `measurement`, the economics domain's `regional`). RCA and location quotients with a declared threshold and the sensitivity over thresholds 0.5 to 2; proximity, density, distance, COI and COG; skill relatedness from labour flows; related and unrelated variety as an entropy partition; the ECI computed through the symmetric matrix D^(−1/2) S D^(−1/2) with the Fiedler-vector reading and the eigengap, and the method of reflections as a power iteration; Fitness–Complexity in the original and in Servedio's stable form; resilience indices (resistance, sensitivity, the shift-share split); scaling exponents with the city's delineation declared; the capability model as a generator of test matrices; prediction as a first-class operation with temporal hold-out and tyche's forecast comparisons (Part IV §2, §3, §7).
13. **NK landscapes** (heuresis's `benchmarks`). Generators with K and the dependency structure declared, with the economic reading (attributes as routines or policies, fitness as profit) and the population-with-selection wrapper in the sub-domain (Part I §5).
14. **Stylised-fact statistics and statistical model checking** (tyche). Subbotin and asymmetric exponential power fits with intervals; tail exponents by Hill's estimator and by maximum likelihood; the Baxter–King filter beside the Hodrick–Prescott filter tyche holds; cross-correlation tables with Monte Carlo bands; persistence as rank autocorrelation and quantile transition matrices; investment spikes; recession durations against an exponential law; run counts chosen sequentially for a stated confidence, warm-up detected, ergodicity diagnosed across and within runs; each stylised fact judged by a declared rule, never a hidden threshold (Part II §3, §4, §7).
15. **A policy portfolio as an evolving population** (an API composition with the policy domain). Instruments or programme variants with budget shares, each carrying a theory of change and a posterior over its effect; fitness as expected net value under the posterior, optionally with the value of information of continuing to fund it; variation by proposed variants with declared rates and missions as a constraint set on admissible variants; selection by a revision protocol over shares (replicator for proportional reallocation, logit with temperature as inertia, Smith for pairwise comparison, BRM for rare reallocation); retention as the experimentalist cycle in kybernesis's architecture; second-order additionality as the difference between coordinated and independent protocols for the same instruments, by simulation. Part III §6 offers this as a design proposal; no source couples these pieces (Part III §6, Gaps).
16. **Bilevel policy search** (an API composition with the allocation domain's evaluators). heuresis's loop as the planner over policies; an inner economy whose agents re-adapt under each candidate; judgement across an ensemble of agent models (rule, genetic-algorithm learning, reinforcement learning, language-model proposals of grade C) and of parameters the record does not identify, by worst case or regret; the costly inner economy screened by the stand-in already built (ADR 0142) (Part V §4, §7).

### 7.4 Data a Model Would Consume

**Table 7.2.** Data, by model or measure. "In the library" names what the repository's data library already reads (from CLAUDE.md); licences marked [M] in the Parts must be verified before use.

| Model or measure | Data | Named in the Parts | In the library | Caveats |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Census records of firms; productivity decompositions | firm or plant panels: identifiers, years, output or value added, employment, capital, intermediate inputs, prices or deflators, entry and exit dates; productivity with matching weights | the Slovenian AJPES panel (Melitz and Polanec); national business registers; Compustat, ORBIS (Part I §7; Part II §7) | none at firm level; Melbourne's CLUE (establishments, employment and floor space by block and industry, 2002–2024) and Market Studio's cafés read as lives give establishments with entry and exit but no productivity | revenue productivity confounds prices; access to registers restricted; results depend on census years |
| Industry life cycles | entrants, exits and incumbents by year and cohort; sizes and survival by cohort | Gort–Klepper series (Part I §7) | CLUE's establishments by kind as a local analogue | the shakeout is a product-level pattern |
| Calibration of Nelson–Winter and K+S | demand elasticity, cost per unit capital, R&D intensities, depreciation, leverage, distributions of productivity growth | Part I §7; the LSD configuration files (Part II §1) | none | the K+S parameter table must come from the `.lsd` files |
| Macro stylised facts | quarterly GDP, consumption, investment, inventories, prices, wages, unemployment, R&D | US NIPA and FRED; OECD (Part II §7) | Eurostat's tables and the World Bank's indicators (annual) | quarterly series needed for the Baxter–King filter |
| Micro stylised facts; entry and exit | firm panels of sales, employment, productivity and investment; business demography | Compustat, ORBIS, registers; Eurostat's structural business statistics and business demography (Part II §7) | none | proprietary panels cannot be redistributed |

No case in this survey or its design rests on a Japanese city. Following the repository's principle that a place is a case of data and never the software's boundary, every reader is written for any place a source covers.

### 7.5 An Order of Building, the Model Side First

The order is set by reuse, by what can be checked exactly, and by the request that the model side come first: analytic models and their checks before simulation models, simulation models before estimation, estimation before connections, and works last. Each stage is checkable before the next rests on it.

1. **The exact core, in the foundations.**
   1. tyche: the census record and the Price decomposition, multilevel, with entry by source, transmission by operator, exit by reason, the second moment and multiple intervals, in exact rationals (checks 1 to 10). This is the piece worth building first: cheap, exact and general, it gives at once a productivity decomposition for any panel of firms and an accounting of what outside proposals contributed to any search (Part V §7).
   2. nomos: the replicator family and revision protocols, with mean dynamics, finite-N processes, positive correlation and Nash stationarity as laws, and the Lyapunov functions (checks 16 to 27).
   3. nomos and tyche: exact stationary distributions, the birth–death formula and the Gibbs distribution (checks 28, 29); the resistance-tree computation after morphe gains a minimum-weight arborescence (check 30).
   4. tyche: urn and growth processes (checks 42 to 44); heuresis: NK landscapes (check 41).
2. **The sub-domain's analytic models and measurements.**
   1. `selection`: the productivity decompositions composed on tyche's record (checks 11 to 15).
   2. `industry`: Metcalfe's and Iwai's population models (checks 5, 35); then Nelson and Winter's chapter 12 model, Klepper's model and Silverberg, Dosi and Orsenigo's, only after their equations are read at the source or in the NELWIN and LSD codes (Part I §7).
   3. `diversification`: the incidence-matrix measures composed on tyche and nomos, with the capability model as test generator and prediction held out in time (checks 45 to 50).
   4. Norms and cultural transmission in nomos, with economic cases (contracts, conventions) composed in the sub-domain (checks 29, 34).
   5. `adaptive`: economies of learning agents (cobweb, Cournot, Kiyotaki and Wright), with the population structure and the election operator declared (checks 36 to 38). This step needs the economics domain to declare heuresis (§7.1, item 3).
3. **Simulation models with published targets.**
   1. The stylised-fact statistics and statistical model checking in tyche (check 55).
   2. The Islands model, the smaller first target with published criteria and surrogate results (check 51).
   3. K+S-original without banks, on the ledger and nomos's agents, held to seeded runs of the LSD code and to the 2014 working paper's policy signs (checks 52 to 54); then banks and policy; then the labour or energy variant (Part II §7).
4. **Estimation and validation.** Screening by Morris on the true model; a surrogate on a Latin hypercube with conformal intervals (heuresis) and Sobol' indices on the surrogate (tyche); simulated minimum distance and simulated-likelihood Bayesian estimation of a reduced set of parameters (tyche's `bayes`); causal structures of VARs on artificial and real data compared (Guerini and Moneta), which needs a causal-search algorithm beside peira's graphs (Part II §4, §7). Every estimate is reported with what the record does not identify, as the earlier survey's Part VIII requires.
5. **Connections, in the API.** The Price decomposition over heuresis's runs; the policy portfolio with the policy domain; bilevel policy search with the allocation domain's evaluators; the experimentalist cycle as a kybernesis architecture; the reading of the generative ecology's gardens (§7.6).
6. **Works.** A view of an industry's genealogy and of a decomposition's terms, and the diversification measures on a map, in an existing work (the Economy Atlas, the Urban Studio or the Dynamics Lab), only after the models beneath pass their checks.

**Deferred.** A Santa Fe-type market, built only with the corrected mutation operator and with invariance to operators reported (Part V §7); populations of language-model agents, used meanwhile only as proposers of grade C validated on the known-answer games (Part V §7); open-endedness measures on quality-diversity runs read as innovation (Part V §7); causal attribution of innovation by counterfactual runs or by a Shapley value over sources, which nomos's Shapley value could compute at a cost the literature has not assessed (Part V §5); Frenken and Boschma's branching model until its equations are read (Part IV §1); the climate variant of K+S (Part II §1).

### 7.6 Connections to the Infrastructure's Frameworks

**heuresis's genealogies and credited proposals.** heuresis's `evolution` records, for every member, the operator or outside source that made it and its parents' tickets, counts proposals and their survivors by source, and credits operators with what their children achieve. These are exactly the census record's operator labels, source labels and parent map, so that a run can be read generation by generation, and over its whole span, into selection, transmission by operator, entry by source and exit by reason (Part V §5, §7). The reading adds what the counts do not give: how much of the change of the population's mean, or of any descriptor, each source's entrants contributed, and how much their descendants contributed later through transmission along lineages. It is an accounting attribution, exact as an identity and not causal (§6.2). Islands, age layers and quality-diversity cells are its levels (Part V §3). Two lessons of evolution as method bind the loop: the population structure (one population shared or one per agent) must be a declared strategy, never a default, since it changes the equilibrium reached (Part V §1); and invariance of a result to operators must be reported, since operator details have decided published results (Part V §1, §6). The allocation domain's searches are a first case: where the rules' orders were proposed to a search's first generation (ADR 0141), the decomposition says what share of the kept orders' improvement entered with those proposals.

**The allocation domain.** Three links. First, its searches are read by the Price decomposition, as above. Second, an evolving economy of two sectors on the ledger is a candidate economic evaluator of allocations whose order matters, beside the economy of places, the law of holdings and learned laws (ADR 0139); it enters only after it passes its checks, and the allocation domain composes it, since it stands on economics. Third, bilevel policy search (§7.2, item 16) uses the allocation domain's stand-in inside the search (ADR 0142) for the costly inner economy, and judges candidates across an ensemble of agent models, as the earlier survey's Part V asked of evaluators. Where a policy is judged under several principles, the allocation domain's verdicts compare it under each without summing them (ADR 0136).

**The policy domain.** An instrument's fitness in a policy portfolio comes from the domain's `evidence`, `transfer` and `options`, and the value of information of continuing to fund it from `adoption` (EVPI, EVSI); selection that rewards both performance and learning is the dual control of active adaptive management in the language of revision protocols (Part III §6). The domain's `learning` (action plans, results on a ladder, contribution claims) is the retention record of a programme's variants. Relatedness, density and complexity enter as evidence of a declared grade through the domain's grading schemes, never as a recommendation (Part IV §6). The composition lives in `science/policy.hpp`, since the two domains stand at the same height.

**kybernesis's steering loop.** Experimentalist governance (framework goals and metrics set jointly, discretion to lower-level units, reports and peer review, revision; Part III §5 [M]) is an architecture declared as data, its turns sealed in archeion's journal through `record`. A revision protocol over budget shares is a method that can be admitted to a block of the loop when kategoria decides that block's laws on a finite tabulation of the method. A practice carried from one jurisdiction to another is a `transfer` by a lens with its support factors, and Volden, Ting and Carpenter's result says that records of adoption alone may not show whether the practice spread by learning (Part III §5). Two inferences of Part III §4 and Part I §4 suit kybernesis's `ontology` and kategoria: an institution kept with two faces, its rule text and the equilibrium beliefs that make it self-enforcing, the mapping between them checked; and the four conditions on interactors (shared dependence, shared organisation, relative independence, property-dependent replication) declared as constraints on a model's units.

**The generative ecology of projects.** Read through the five Parts, the ecology's rungs are kinds of selection. A garden of persistent lineages that are retired and not divided (rung 2) undergoes subset selection, a contraction that runs dry unless founders and channels replenish it; division with variation and reseeding (rung 3) is generative selection (Part I §4). A garden's progress can be decomposed by the census record: selection among lineages by the conserved resource they hold, transmission by patch and by reseeding as two operators, entry by founder and channel as sources, exit by retirement with its reason, and gardens as a level when lineages migrate (rung 4). Nelson and Winter's selection has no appointed evaluator: fitness is the capital growth that a market produces, and depends on the population through the price (Part I §1); this is the economic model of selection without an appointed judge that the ecology's design asks for. Three further points are this survey's inferences, offered to the framework's author and not claimed for the framework: its first proposition (selection acts through what payers distinguish) reads, in Price's terms, as the statement that only characters covarying with what payers reward change by selection, all others by transmission alone; the group within which a lineage's payoff is compared changes what is selected, as Vriend's two kinds of learning show (Part V §1); and the replicator's formal identity with Bayes's rule, fitness as likelihood (Part V §3 [M]), relates a focus held as a prior to selection as its update.

**Other foundations.** kinesis's `transitions` give regional "ecological resilience" a dynamical reading stronger than the shift-share indices of evolutionary economic geography (Part IV §1); tyche's forecast comparisons serve the prediction of entry and of growth by relatedness and complexity (Part IV §7); peira's designs are needed before any claim that selection on productivity, or a smart-specialisation strategy, caused an outcome (Part I §6; Part IV §6); archeion seals census records and runs; kategoria checks typed records and the mapping of institutions.

### 7.7 What Must Stay a Person's Judgement, and What a Tool Must Flag

**What stays with people.** The Parts name the choices that carry the content of an evolutionary model and must not be fixed silently by a default.

- **The units and their genealogy:** what counts as a unit (firm, plant, routine, rule, lineage), who descends from whom (continuing identity, spinoffs, mergers, imitation), and at which levels groups are declared (Part I §4; Part V §5).
- **The terms of a decomposition:** the weight (employment, output, value added, capacity), the character (revenue or physical productivity), the reference for entry and exit, the census years, logs or levels (Part I §3; Part V §5).
- **The structure of learning:** whether agents learn in one population or each in their own, the operators, mutation rates, learning speeds and election, which the record does not identify and whose range must be shown (Part V §1, §6).
- **The terms of validation:** which stylised facts, which statistics, which acceptance rule (Part II §3, §7).
- **The terms of measurement:** RCA thresholds, classification levels, the delineation of cities, the relatedness matrix chosen (Part IV §3, §7).
- **The values of policy:** the social welfare function, the worth of outcomes, the directionality of missions, the admissible variants, and the final choice among policies, which a person makes with reasons recorded through the allocation domain's sealed procedure (Part III §5, §6; Part V §4).

**What a tool must flag in every report:**

- a decomposition over census intervals that do not add, or against a reference that over-measures entry (Part I §3; Part V §5);
- a selection term read from revenue productivity (Part I §3);
- an ECI with a small eigengap, or results that change across RCA thresholds; a Fitness–Complexity map with zeros (Part IV §3);
- a result not shown invariant to the operators (Part V §6);
- a Monte Carlo comparison without controlled run counts and warm-up, or an average across runs where paths depend on history (Part II §1, §4);
- parameters the record does not identify (Part V §6; the earlier survey's Part VIII);
- the share of behaviour generated by language models (the earlier survey's Part VI);
- any model equation or check resting on a source marked [M] (§0.3).

### 7.8 What a Tool Must Never Claim

The prohibitions of the five Parts share one form: presenting an accounting, a simulation or an association as a cause, a law or a recommendation. Gathered:

1. That a decomposition's selection term shows that the character caused growth. The terms are descriptive; a claim of selection on productivity needs a causal design or a structural model (Part I §6).
2. That an entry or exit contribution is independent of its reference, or that decompositions over different census years agree (Part I §3; Part V §5).
3. That Fisher's theorem holds where the transmission term does not vanish, or that Price's equation predicts (Part I §2; Part V §3, §6).
4. That reproducing stylised facts validates a model or identifies its mechanisms, that matching statistical dependencies settles its causal structure, or that a policy result at one calibrated point holds elsewhere (Part II §3, §4, §6).
5. That an evolutionary macro model is estimated when a few parameters were fitted on chosen moments (Part II §4).
6. That a result of genetic-algorithm learning is a property of the economy when it turns on an operator, a learning speed or the population structure (Part V §1, §6).
7. That a rule or strategy found by search has an edge without evaluation held out and costs counted (Part V §2).
8. That the ECI of a region counts its capabilities, or that density or complexity recommends a diversification; that smart specialisation or a mission caused an outcome (Part IV §3, §6; Part III §5).
9. That a Zipf tail reveals its mechanism, Simon's process and Kesten's giving the same tail, or that preferential attachment is the default mechanism for technological components (Part IV §4, §5).
10. That an observed norm is the stochastically stable one on the evidence of a cross-section, or that the diffusion of a policy shows learning from others (Part III §4, §5).
11. That the learning rule that fits best is the rule people use (Part III §3).
12. That language-model agents behave as people do, or that a policy designed against simulated agents transfers to a real economy (Part V §4).
13. That entry by source is causal credit for outside proposals (Part V §5).

## 8. References

Each entry carries the strongest marker any Part gave it and the Parts that cite it; a DOI or detail marked [M] in every Part that gives it stays [M] and must be checked before citation. Works cited only inside another source's reference list are marked [V ref] where the source was read. Web pages, codes and databases follow the publications.

### 8.2 Codes, Models and Databases

- LSD, the Laboratory for Simulation Development (Valente; Pereira), GPL. [GitHub marcov64/lsd](https://github.com/marcov64/lsd). [S]. With the K+S family, the industry model and the Islands model in [SantAnnaKS/LSD](https://www.ews.santannapisa.it/), branch `8.0-stable`, `Example/SantAnna/K+S-original` [V]. Part II §1, §5, §7.
- LSD-GIS (Schaff). [Zenodo](https://zenodo.org/records/4661460). [S]. Part II §5.
- Eurace@Unibi in FLAME. [GitHub ETACE/eurace_unibi](https://github.com/ETACE/eurace_unibi); [model page](https://www.uni-bielefeld.de/fakultaeten/wirtschaftswissenschaften/lehrbereiche/etace/eurace@unibi/the-eurace@unibi-model/) [V]. Part II §2, §5.
- JAS-mine MacroABM (Lhuillier 2016; Richardson 2018). [Page](https://www.microsimulation.ac.uk/jas-mine/demo/macro-model) [V]; [GitHub jasmineRepo/MacroABM](https://www.github.com/jasmineRepo/MacroABM). Part II §2, §5.
- MultiVeStA, a statistical model checker. [Page](https://sysma.imtlucca.it/tools/tool_multivesta/). [S]. Part II §4, §5.
- Mesa, ABCE, JMAB; CoMSES Net. [M]; [CoMSES record of the 2010 K+S paper](https://catalog.comses.net/publications/1770) [S]. Part II §5.
- Andersen's NELWIN reconstructions of Nelson and Winter. [S]. Part I §1, §7.
- QuantEcon's lecture on Marimon, McGrattan and Sargent, and the replication project. [Lecture](https://python.quantecon.org/marimon_mcgrattan_sargent.html); [replication](https://mmcky.github.io/paper-marimon-mcgrattan-sargent-1990/). [S]. Part V §1, §7.
- Empirical, Phylotrack and hereditary stratigraphy, tools for lineage and phylogeny. [S]. Part V §5.
- Data named in Table 7.2 (BACI, the Growth Lab's trade data, Comtrade, County Business Patterns, QCEW, Eurostat's structural business statistics and business demography, BRES via Nomis, LEHD, O*NET, OEWS, IPUMS, PatentsView, REGPAT, Google Patents, PATSTAT, OpenAlex, NETS, Overture Maps, OpenStreetMap, the GHSL Urban Centre Database, BEA and OECD metropolitan output, US NIPA and FRED, Compustat, ORBIS, Laeven and Valencia's database, the Illinois share-contract survey, the OECD's database of missions): licences and access [M] in the Parts, to be verified before use. Part II §7; Part III §4, §5; Part IV §7.
- Secondary pages relied on: [Wikipedia, NK model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NK_model) [S] (Part I §5); [Wikipedia, genetic algorithms in economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_algorithms_in_economics) [S] (Part V §1); [EITM lecture notes on the cobweb](https://www.uh.edu/hobby/eitm/_docs/past-lectures/2014-Lectures/Sunny-Wong/Genetic-Algorithm-Learning-and-the-Cobweb-Model.pdf) [S] (Part V §1); [Tesfatsion's overview of the Santa Fe market](https://www2.econ.iastate.edu/tesfatsi/SFIStockMarketModel.LTOverview.pdf) [S] (Part V §1); [the Stafforini catalogue page of Nelson and Winter](https://stafforini.com/works/nelson-1982-evolutionary-theory-economic/) [S] (Part I §1).
